\textstyle{\pi_{n+1}(\mathbf{K}_{\Nil}^{\infty}(\mathcal{A}[\mathbb{Z}],\Phi[\mathbb{Z}]))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s}
\textstyle{\pi_{n}(\mathbf{K}^{\infty}(\mathcal{A}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi_{n}(\mathbf{K}_{\Nil}^{\infty}(\mathcal{A},\Phi))}

where
r\circ i=\id
and
j\circ s=\id
and these maps are part of the corresponding
(untwisted) Bass-Heller-Swan decompositions. Iterating this, one obtains for every
m\geq 0
a commutative diagram

\textstyle{\pi_{n}(\mathbf{K}^{\infty}(\mathcal{A}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{\pi_{n}(\mathbf{K}_{\Nil}^{\infty}(\mathcal{A},\Phi))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j}
\textstyle{\pi_{n+m}(\mathbf{K}^{\infty}(\mathcal{A}[\mathbb{Z}^{m}]))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{r}
\textstyle{\pi_{n+m}(\mathbf{K}_{\Nil}^{\infty}(\mathcal{A}[\mathbb{Z}^{m}],\Phi[\mathbb{Z}^{m}]))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s}
\textstyle{\pi_{n}(\mathbf{K}^{\infty}(\mathcal{A}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi_{n}(\mathbf{K}_{\Nil}^{\infty}(\mathcal{A},\Phi))}

where
r\circ i=\id
and
j\circ s=\id
holds. Now choose
m
such that
n+m\geq 1
holds.
Then the middle horizontal arrow can be identified by construction with its connective version

\pi_{n+m}(\mathbf{K}(\mathcal{A}[\mathbb{Z}^{m}]))\to\pi_{n+m}(\mathbf{K}(\Nil(\mathcal{A}[\mathbb{Z}^{m}],\Phi[\mathbb{Z}^{m}]))).

Since this map is a bijection by
Lemma
7.3

the upper horizontal arrow is a retract of an isomorphism and hence itself an isomorphism.
∎

Theorem 7.8

(The non-connective
K
-theory of additive categories)
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an additive category.
Suppose that
\mathcal{A}[\mathbb{Z}^{m}]

is regular coherent for every
m\geq 0
.
Consider any automorphism
\Phi\colon\mathcal{A}\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathcal{A}
of additive categories.

Then we get a weak homotopy equivalence of non-connective spectra

\mathbf{a}^{\infty}\colon\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{K}^{\infty}(\Phi^{-1})}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathbf{K}^{\infty}(\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t,t^{-1}]).

Proof.

This follows from Theorem
4.1
,
since Lemma
7.7

implies
\pi_{n}(\mathbf{E}^{\infty}(R,\Phi))=0
and hence

\pi_{n}(\mathbf{N}\mathbf{K}^{\infty}(R_{\Phi}[t]))=\pi_{n}(\mathbf{N}\mathbf{K}^{\infty}(\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t^{-1}]))=0
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.
∎

8.
Noetherian additive categories

Theorem 8.1

(Hilbert Basis Theorem for additive categories)
.

Consider an additive category
\mathcal{A}
together with an automorphism

\Phi\colon\mathcal{A}\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathcal{A}
.

(i)

If the additive category
\mathcal{A}
is Noetherian, then the additive
categories
\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t]
,
\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t^{-1}]
, and
\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t,t^{-1}]
are
Noetherian;

(ii)

If the additive category
\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t]
is Noetherian, then the additive
category
\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t,t^{-1}]
is Noetherian.

Proof.

(i)
We
only treat
\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t]
, the proof for
\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t^{-1}]
is analogous. For

\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t,t^{-1}]
the claim will follow then
from
(ii)
.

We translate the usual proof of the Hilbert Basis Theorem for rings to additive categories.
Consider a finitely generated
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t]
-module
N
and a

\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t]
-submodule
M\subseteq N
. We have to show that
M
is finitely
generated. Lemma
5.4

(vi)
implies
that there is an epimorphisms
\phi\colon\mor_{\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t]}(?,A)\to N
for some
object
A
. If
\phi^{-1}(M)
is finitely generated, then
M
is finitely generated,
since
f
induces an epimorphism
f^{-1}(M)\to M
. Hence we can assume without loss of
generality
N=\mor_{\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t]}(?,A)
.

Fix an object
Z
in
\mathcal{A}
. Consider a non-trivial element
f\colon Z\to A
in

N(Z)
. We can write it as a finite sum
\sum_{k=0}^{d(f)}f_{k}\cdot t^{k}
, where

f_{k}\colon\Phi^{k}(Z)\to A
is a morphism in
\mathcal{A}
and
f_{d(f)}\not=0
. We call
the natural number
d(f)
the
degree
of
f
and

R(f)=f_{d(f)}\colon\Phi^{d(f)}(Z)\to A
the
leading coefficient
of
f
. We
put
d(0\colon Z\to A)=-\infty
and
R(0\colon Z\to A)=0
.

We define now
I_{d}
as the
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}
-submodule of
\mor_{\mathcal{A}}(?,A)
that is generated by all

R(f)
with
f\in M(Z)
and
d(f)=d
for some object
Z
from
\mathcal{A}
.
We have
I_{0}\subseteq I_{1}\subseteq I_{2}\subseteq\cdots
and
define
I
to be the
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}
-submodule
\bigcup_{d\geq 0}I_{d}
. As
\mathcal{A}
is by
assumption Noetherian,
I
and all the
I_{d}
are finitely generated. Therefore we find a
finite collection of morphisms

f_{i}\in M(Z_{i})\subseteq\mor_{\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t]}(Z_{i},\mathcal{A})
such that the
R(f_{i})

generate
I
. We abbreviate
d_{i}:=d(f_{i})
. Since each
f_{i}
lies in of the
I_{d}
-s,
we can find a natural number
d_{0}
such that

I=I_{d_{0}}=I_{d}
holds for
d\geq d_{0}
. Hence we can also arrange for the

f_{i}
to have the following property: for each
d
the
R(f_{i})
with
d_{i}\leq d

generate
I_{d}
. We record that

R(f_{i})\in I_{d_{i}}(\Phi^{d_{i}}(Z_{i}))\subseteq\mor_{\mathcal{A}}(\Phi^{d_{i}}(Z_{i}),A)
.

We will show that the
f_{i}
generate
M
.
Let
f\in M(Z)
,
f\neq 0
. We abbreviate
d:=d(f)
.
We have
R(f)\in I_{d}(\Phi^{d}(Z))\subseteq\mor_{\mathcal{A}}(\Phi^{d}(Z),A)
.
We can write

R(f)=\sum_{i}R(f_{i})\circ\varphi_{i}

with
\varphi_{i}\in\mor_{\mathcal{A}}(\Phi^{d}(Z),\Phi^{d_{i}}(Z_{i})
and
\varphi_{i}=0
whenever
d(f_{i})>d(f)
.
Set

\tilde{\varphi}_{i}:={\Phi}^{-d_{i}}(\varphi_{i})\cdot t^{d-d_{i}}\in\mor_{\mathcal{A}_{\phi}[t]}(Z,Z_{i}).

Then

R\Big(\sum_{i}f_{i}\circ\tilde{\varphi}_{i}\Big)=\sum_{i}R(f_{i})\circ\Phi^{d_{i}}(\Phi^{-d_{i}}(\varphi_{i}))=\sum_{i}R(f_{i})\circ\varphi_{i}.

Thus
d(f-\sum_{i}f_{i}\circ\tilde{\varphi})<d
. Now we can repeat the argument for

f^{\prime}:=f-\sum_{i}f_{i}\circ\tilde{\varphi}
. By induction on
d(f)
we now find that

f
belongs to the submodule of
\mor_{\mathcal{A}_{\phi}[t](?,A)}
generated by the
f_{i}
.
Hence
M
is a finitely generated
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t]
-module.

(ii)

It suffices to show for a
\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t,t^{-1}]
-submodule
M
of

\mor_{\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t,t^{-1}]}(?,A)
that
M
is finitely generated as

\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t,t^{-1}]
-module. For
Z\in\mathcal{A}
we have

\mor_{\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t]}(Z,A)\subseteq\mor_{\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t,t^{-1}]}(Z,A)
. We define the

\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t]
-module
M^{\prime}
by

M^{\prime}(Z):=M(Z)\cap\mor_{\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t]}(Z,A).

Since
\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t]
is Noetherian, we find a finite collection of morphisms

f_{i}\in M^{\prime}(Z_{i})\subseteq M(Z_{i})\subseteq\mor_{\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t,t^{-1}]}(Z_{i},A)
that
generate
M^{\prime}
as an
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t]
-module. We claim that the
f_{i}
also generate

M
as an
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t,t^{-1}]
-module. Let

f\in M(Z)\subseteq\mor_{\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t,t^{-1}]}(Z,A)
. For
d\geq 0
we have

\id_{Z}\cdot t^{d}\in\mor_{\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t,t^{-1}]}(\Phi^{-d}(Z),Z)
. For sufficiently
large
d
we have

f\circ(\id_{Z}\cdot t^{d})\in\mor_{\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t]}(\Phi^{-d}(Z),A)\cap M(\Phi^{-d}(Z))=M^{\prime}(\Phi^{-d}(Z))
. Thus
f\circ(\id_{Z}\cdot t^{d})
belongs to the

\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t,t^{-1}]
-submodule of
M
generated by the
f_{i}
. As

(\id_{Z}\cdot t^{d})
is an isomorphism in
\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t,t^{-1}]
,
f
also belongs to
the
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t,t^{-1}]
-submodule of
M
generated by the
f_{i}
.
∎

9.
Additive categories with finite global dimension

Let
\Phi\colon\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}
be an automorphism of the additive category
\mathcal{A}
.
Let
\overline{\Phi}\colon\mathcal{A}_{\phi}[t]\to\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t]
be the automorphism of
additive categories induced by
\Phi
, which sends the morphisms

\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}f_{k}\cdot t^{k}\colon A\to B
to the morphism

\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\Phi(f_{k})\cdot t^{k}\colon\Phi(A)\to\Phi(B)
. Denote
by
i\colon\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t]
the inclusion sending
f\colon A\to B
to

(f\cdot t^{0})\colon A\to B
. Obviously we have

\overline{\Phi}\circ i=i\circ\Phi
.

9.
a
.
The characteristic sequence

Consider a
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t]
-module
M
. Let

e\colon i_{*}i^{*}M\to M

be the
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t]
-morphism, which is the adjoint of the
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}
-homomorphism

\id\colon i^{*}M\to i^{*}M
under the adjunction (
5.7
). We
get for every object
A
in
\mathcal{A}
a morphism

\id_{\Phi(A)}\cdot t\colon A\to\Phi(A)
in
\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t]
. It induces a

\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t]
-morphism
M(\id_{\phi(A)}\cdot t)\colon M(\Phi(A))\to M(A)
. Since for a morphisms

u\colon A\to B
in
\mathcal{A}
we have

(\id_{\phi(B)}\cdot t)\circ i(u)=(\id_{\phi(B)}\cdot t)\circ(u\cdot t^{0})=\Phi(u)\cdot t\\
=(\Phi(u)\cdot t^{0})\circ(\id_{\Phi(A)}\cdot t)=i(\Phi(u))\circ(\id_{\Phi(A)}\cdot t),

we obtain a morphism of
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}
-modules

(9.1)

\alpha^{\prime}\colon\Phi^{*}i^{*}M\xrightarrow{\cong}i^{*}M.

By applying
i_{*}
we
obtain a morphism of
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t]
-modules

\alpha\colon i_{*}\Phi^{*}i^{*}M\to i_{*}i^{*}M.

The morphism
\id_{\Phi(A)}\cdot t\colon A\to\Phi(A)
in
\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t]
induces also a

\mathbb{Z}
-map

\beta(A)\colon i_{*}\Phi^{*}i^{*}M(A)=i_{*}i^{*}M(\Phi(A))\to i_{*}i^{*}M(A).

Since for any morphism

v=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}f_{k}\cdot t^{k}\colon A\to B

in
\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t]
we have

\displaystyle(\id_{\Phi(B)}\cdot t)\circ v

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\id_{\Phi(B)}\cdot t)\circ\biggl(\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}f_{k}\cdot t^{k}\biggr)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(\id_{\Phi(B)}\cdot t)\circ(f_{k}\cdot t^{k})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\Phi(f_{k})\cdot t^{k+1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(\Phi(f_{k})\cdot t^{k})\circ(\id_{\Phi(A)}\cdot t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\overline{\Phi}\biggl(\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}f_{k}\cdot t^{k}\biggr)\circ(\id_{\Phi(A)}\cdot t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\overline{\Phi}(v)\circ(\id_{\Phi(A)}\cdot t),

we get a
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t]
-homomorphism
denoted by

\beta\colon i_{*}\Phi^{*}i^{*}M\to i_{*}i^{*}M.

Define the so called
characteristic sequence
of

\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}_{\phi}[t]
-modules by

(9.2)

0\to i_{*}\Phi^{*}i^{*}M\xrightarrow{\alpha-\beta}i_{*}i^{*}M\xrightarrow{e}M\to 0.

Given an object
A\in\mathcal{A}
,
(\alpha-\beta)(A)
is explicitly given by

M(\Phi(?))\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}}\mor_{\mathcal{A}_{\phi}[t]}(A,{?})\to M(?)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}}\mor_{\mathcal{A}_{\phi}[t]}(A,{?}),\\
x\otimes(f_{k}\cdot t^{k}\colon A\to{?})\mapsto M(\id_{\Phi(?)}\cdot t\colon{?}\to\Phi(?))(x)\otimes(f_{k}\cdot t^{k}\colon A\to{?})\\
-x\otimes(\Phi(f_{k})\cdot t^{k+1}\colon A\to\Phi(?)),

and
e(A)
is explicitly given by

M(?)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}}\mor_{\mathcal{A}_{\phi}[t]}(A,?)\to M(A),\quad x\otimes(u\colon A\to{?})\mapsto M(u)(x)=xu.

Lemma 9.3
.

The characteristic sequence (
9.2
)
is natural in
M
and exact.

Proof.

It is obviously natural in
M
.
To prove exactness, it suffices to prove the exactness of the sequence of
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}
-modules

(9.4)

0\to i^{*}i_{*}\Phi^{*}i^{*}M\xrightarrow{\alpha-\beta}i^{*}i_{*}i^{*}M\xrightarrow{e}i^{*}M\to 0.

Let
N
be a
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}
-module. We obtain a
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}
-isomorphism

(9.5)

S(N)\colon\bigoplus_{k=0}^{\infty}\Phi^{k}(N)\xrightarrow{\cong}i^{*}i_{*}N,

which is defined for an object
A
in
\mathcal{A}
by the
\mathbb{Z}
-isomorphism

S(N)(A)\colon\bigoplus_{k=0}^{\infty}N(\Phi^{k}(A))\xrightarrow{\cong}i^{*}i_{*}N(A)=N(?)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}}\mor_{\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}{t}}(i(A),?)

sending
(x_{k})_{k\geq 0}
to
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}x\otimes\bigl(\id_{\Phi^{k}(A)}\cdot t^{k}\colon A\to\Phi^{k}(A)\bigr)
.
The inverse of
S(N)(A)

sends
y\otimes\biggl(\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}f_{k}\cdot t^{k}\colon A\to{?}\bigr)
to

\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}N(f_{k})(y)
.
Applying this to
N=i^{*}\overline{\Phi}^{*}M=\Phi^{*}i^{*}M
and
N=i^{*}M
, we get identifications

\displaystyle i^{*}i_{*}i^{*}\overline{\Phi}^{*}M=\bigoplus_{k=1}^{\infty}(\Phi^{k})^{*}i^{*}M;

\displaystyle i^{*}i_{*}i^{*}M=\bigoplus_{k=0}^{\infty}(\Phi^{k})^{*}i^{*}M.

Consider natural numbers
m
and
n
with
m\geq n
. For an object
A
let the map

s_{m,n}(A)\colon(\overline{\Phi}^{m})^{*}M(A)\to(\overline{\Phi}^{n})^{*}M(A)
be the map
obtained by applying
M
to the morphism

\id_{\Phi^{m}(A)}\cdot t^{m-n}\colon\Phi^{n}(A)\to\Phi^{m}(A)
in
\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t]
. This
yields a map of
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}
-modules

s_{m,n}\colon(\Phi^{m})^{*}i^{*}M\to(\Phi^{n})^{*}i^{*}M.

Under these identifications the
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}
-sequence (
9.4
)
becomes the sequence

0\to\bigoplus_{m=1}^{\infty}(\Phi^{m})^{*}i^{*}M\xrightarrow{\begin{pmatrix}-s_{1,0}&0&0&0&\cdots\\
\id&-s_{2,1}&0&0&\cdots\\
0&\id&-s_{3,2}&0&\cdots\\
0&0&\id&-s_{4,3}&\cdots\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\end{pmatrix}}\bigoplus_{n=0}^{\infty}(\Phi^{n})^{*}i^{*}M\\
\xrightarrow{\begin{pmatrix}\id&s_{1,0}&s_{2,0}&\cdots\end{pmatrix}}i^{*}M\to 0.

Since
s_{m,n}\circ s_{l,m}=s_{l,n}
for
l\geq m\geq n
and
s_{m,m}=\id
hold, this
sequence is split exact, with a splitting given by

\bigoplus_{m=1}^{\infty}(\Phi^{m})^{*}M\xleftarrow{\begin{pmatrix}0&\id&s_{2,1}&s_{3,1}&s_{4,1}&\cdots\\
0&0&\id&s_{3,2}&s_{4,2}&\cdots\\
0&0&0&\id&s_{4,3}&\cdots\\
0&0&0&0&\id&\cdots\\
0&0&0&0&0&\cdots\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\end{pmatrix}}\bigoplus_{n=0}^{\infty}(\Phi^{n})^{*}M\xleftarrow{\begin{pmatrix}\id\\
0\\
0\\
0\\
0\\
\vdots\end{pmatrix}}M.

∎

9.
b
.
Localization

Definition 9.6

(Local module)
.

We call a
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t]
-module
M

local
, if for any object
A
in
\mathcal{A}

and any natural number
k\in\mathbb{N}
the map

M(\id_{\Phi^{k}(A)}\cdot t^{k})\colon M(A)\to M(\Phi^{k}(A))

induced by the morphism
\id_{\Phi^{k}(A)}\cdot t^{k}\colon A\to\Phi^{k}(A)
in

\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t]
is bijective.

Let
j\colon\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t]\to\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t,t^{-1}]
be the inclusion.

Lemma 9.7
.

A
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t]
-module
M

is local, if and only if there is a
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t,t^{-1}]
-module
N

such that
M
and
j^{*}N
are isomorphic as
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t]
-modules.

Proof.

Since the morphism
\id_{\Phi^{k}(A)}\cdot t^{k}\colon A\to\Phi^{k}(A)
in

\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t]
becomes invertible when considered in
\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t,t^{-1}]
,
a
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t]
-module
M
is local, if there is a
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t,t^{-1}]
-module
N

such that
M
and
j^{*}N
are isomorphic as
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t,t^{-1}]
-modules.

Now consider a local
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t]
-module
M
. We have to explain how the

\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t]
-structure extends to a
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t,t^{-1}]
-structure.
Consider a morphism
u\colon A\to B
in
\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t,t^{-1}]
. Then we can choose a
natural number
m
such that the composite

A\xrightarrow{u}B\xrightarrow{\id_{\Phi^{m}(B)}\cdot t^{m}}\Phi^{m}(B)
is a morphism in

\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t]
. Hence we have the
\mathbb{Z}
-map

M((\id_{\Phi^{m}(B)}\cdot t^{m})\circ u)\colon M(\Phi^{m}(B))\to M(A)
. Since
M
is local,
the
\mathbb{Z}
-map
M(\id_{\Phi^{m}(B)}\cdot t^{m})\colon M(\Phi^{m}(B))\to M(B)
is a
isomorphism. Now define

M(u)\colon M(B)\xrightarrow{M(\id_{\Phi^{m}(B)}\cdot t^{m})^{-1}}M(\Phi^{m}(B))\xrightarrow{M((\id_{\Phi^{m}(B)}\cdot t^{m})\circ u)}M(A).

We leave it to the reader to check that the definition of
M(u)
is independent of the
choice of
m
and that we obtain the desired
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t,t^{-1}]
-structure on
M

extending the given
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t]
-structure.
∎

Let
M
be a
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t]
-module. We want to assign to it a

\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t,t^{-1}]
-module
S^{-1}M
as follows. Consider an object
A
in

\mathcal{A}
. Define the abelian group

S^{-1}M(A):=\{(l,x)\mid l\in\mathbb{Z},x\in M(\Phi^{l}(A))\}/\sim

for the equivalence relation
\sim
, where
(l_{0},x_{0})
and
(l_{1},x_{1})
are equivalent, if
and only if there is an integer
l\in\mathbb{Z}
with
l\leq l_{0},l_{1}
such that the elements

M(\id_{\Phi^{l_{0}}(A)}\cdot t^{l_{0}-l})(x_{0})
and

M(\id_{\Phi^{l_{1}}(A)}\cdot t^{l_{1}-l})(x_{0})
of
M(\Phi^{l}(A))
agree. Given a morphism

u\colon A\to B
in
\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t,t^{-1}]
, we can choose a natural number
m
such
that the composite
A\xrightarrow{u}B\xrightarrow{\id_{\Phi^{m}(B)}\cdot t^{m}}\Phi^{m}(B)

is a morphism in
\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t]
. Define

S^{-1}(M)(u)\colon S^{-1}(M)(B)\to S^{-1}M(A)
by sending
[l,x]

to the class of

\bigl(l-m,M\bigl(\overline{\Phi}^{l-m}((\id_{\Phi^{m}(B)}\cdot t^{m})\circ u)\bigr)(x)\bigr)
. This is independent of the choice of the representative of
[l,x]
,
since we get for the different representative
(l-1,M(\id_{\Phi^{l}(B)}\cdot t)(x))

\displaystyle S^{-1}(M)([l-1,M(\id_{\Phi^{l}(B)}\cdot t)(x)])

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigl[l-1-m,M\bigl(\overline{\Phi}^{l-1-m}((\id_{\Phi^{m}(B)}\cdot t^{m})\circ u)\bigr)\circ M(\id_{\Phi^{l}(B)}\cdot t)(x)\bigr]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigl[l-1-m,M\bigl((\id_{\Phi^{l}(B)}\cdot t)\circ\overline{\Phi}^{l-1-m}((\id_{\Phi^{m}(B)}\cdot t^{m})\circ u)\bigr)(x)\bigr]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigl[l-1-m,M\bigl((\id_{\Phi^{l}(B)}\cdot t)\circ(\id_{\Phi^{l-1(B)}}\cdot t^{m})\circ\overline{\Phi}^{l-1-m}(u)\bigr)(x)\bigr]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigl[l-1-m,M\bigl((\id_{\Phi^{l}(B)}\cdot t^{m}\circ\id_{\phi^{l-m}(B)}\cdot t)\circ\overline{\Phi}^{l-1-m}(u)\bigr)(x)\bigr]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigl[l-1-m,M\bigl((\id_{\Phi^{l}(B)}\cdot t^{m})\circ\overline{\Phi}^{l-m}(u)\bigr)(x)\bigr]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle S^{-1}(M)([l,x]).

This is independent of the choice of
m
by the following calculation

\displaystyle\bigl[l-(m+1),M\bigl(\overline{\Phi}^{l-(m+1)}((\id_{\Phi^{m+1}(B)}\cdot t^{m+1})\circ u)\bigr)(x)\bigr]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigl[l-(m+1),M\bigl(\overline{\Phi}^{l-(m+1)}(\id_{\Phi^{m+1}(B)}\cdot t^{m+1})\circ\overline{\Phi}^{l-(m+1)}(u)\bigr)(x)\bigr]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigl[l-(m+1),M\bigl((\id_{\Phi^{l}(B)}\cdot t^{m+1})\circ\overline{\Phi}^{l-(m+1)}(u)\bigr)(x)\bigr]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigl[l-m-1,M\bigl((\id_{\Phi^{l}(B)}\cdot t^{m})\circ(\id_{\Phi^{l-m}(B)}\cdot t)\circ\overline{\Phi}^{l-m-1}(u)\bigr)(x)\bigr]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigl[l-m-1,M\bigl((\id_{\Phi^{l}(B)}\cdot t^{m})\circ\overline{\Phi}^{l-m}(u)\circ(\id_{\Phi^{l-m}(B)}\cdot t)\bigr)(x)\bigr]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigl[l-m-1,M\bigl(\overline{\Phi}^{l-m}(\id_{\Phi^{m}(B)}\cdot t^{m})\circ\overline{\Phi}^{l-m}(u)\circ(\id_{\Phi^{l-m}(B)}\cdot t)\bigr)(x)\bigr]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigl[l-m-1,M\bigl(\overline{\Phi}^{l-m}((\id_{\Phi^{m}(B)}\cdot t^{m})\circ u)\circ(\id_{\Phi^{l-m}(B)}\cdot t)\bigr)(x)\bigr]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigl[l-m-1,M(\id_{\phi^{l-m}(B)}\cdot t)\circ M\bigl(\overline{\Phi}^{l-m}((\id_{\Phi^{m}(B)}\cdot t^{m})\circ u)\bigr)(x)\bigr]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigl[l-m,M\bigl(\overline{\Phi}^{l-m}((\id_{\Phi^{m}(B)}\cdot t^{m})\circ u)\bigr)(x)\bigr].

We leave it to the reader to check that
S^{-1}M(v\circ u)=S^{-1}M(u)\circ S^{-1}M(v)

holds for any two composable morphisms
u\colon A\to B
and
v\colon B\to C
in

\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t,t^{-1}]
and
S^{-1}M(\id_{A})=\id_{S^{-1}M(A)}
holds for any object
A

in
\mathcal{A}
. Note that the
\mathcal{A}_{\phi}[t]
-module
j^{*}S^{-1}M
is local by
Lemma
9.7

There is a natural map of
\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t]
-modules

I\colon M\to j^{*}S^{-1}M,

which is given for an object
A
of
\mathcal{A}
by the map
I(A)\colon M(A)\to S^{-1}M(A)

sending
x
to
(0,x)
. We claim that
I
is
a localization
in the sense that for any
local
\mathcal{A}_{\phi}[t]
-module
N
and any
\mathcal{A}_{\phi}[t]
-homomorphism

f\colon M\to N
there exists precisely one
\mathcal{A}_{\phi}[t]
-homomorphism

S^{-1}f\colon S^{-1}M\to N
.

Firstly we explain that there is at most one such map
S^{-1}f
with these properties.
Namely, consider an object
A\in\mathcal{A}
and an element
[m,x]\in S^{-1}(M)(A)
. If

m\geq 0
, then we compute

(9.8)

\displaystyle S^{-1}f(A)([m,x])

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle S^{-1}(A)([0,M(\id_{\Phi^{m}(A)}\cdot t^{m})(x)])

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle S^{-1}(A)\circ I(A)\circ M(\id_{\Phi^{m}(A)}\cdot t^{m})(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f(A)\circ M(\id_{\Phi^{m}(A)}\cdot t^{m})(x).

Suppose
m\leq 0
.
Since we have
S^{-1}(M)(\id_{A}\cdot t^{-m})([m,x])=[0,x]
,
we compute for
[m,x]\in S^{-1}M(A)

\displaystyle S^{-1}(N)(\id_{A}\cdot t^{-m})\circ S^{-1}f(A)([m,x])

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle S^{-1}f(\Phi^{m}(A))\circ S^{-1}(M)(\id_{A}\cdot t^{-m})([m,x])

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle S^{-1}f(\Phi^{m}(A))([0,x])

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle S^{-1}f(\Phi^{m}(A))\circ I(A)(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f(\Phi^{m}(A))(x).

Since the locality of
N
implies that

S^{-1}(N)(\id_{\phi^{m}(A)}\cdot t^{m})
is an isomorphism, we conclude

(9.9)

S^{-1}f(A)([m,x])=S^{-1}(N)(\id_{A}\cdot t^{-m})^{-1}\circ f(\Phi^{m}(A))(x).

Hence
S^{-1}f(A)
is determined by the equations (
9.8
)
and (
9.9
). We leave it to the reader to check that it makes
sense to define the desired
\mathbb{Z}_{\mathcal{A}}[t]
-homomorphism
S^{-1}f(A)
by the
equations (
9.8
) and (
9.9
).

The adjoint of
I\colon M\to j^{*}S^{-1}M
under the adjunction (
5.7
)
is denoted by

(9.10)

\alpha\colon j_{*}M\to S^{-1}M.

The adjoint of
\id_{j_{*}M}
under the adjunction (
5.7
)
is the
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t]
-homomorphism

(9.11)

\lambda\colon M\to j^{*}j_{*}M,

which is explicitly given by

M(??)\to\mor_{\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t,t^{-1}]}(??,?)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t]}M(?)

sending
u\in M(??)
to
\id_{??}\otimes u
.
Given an
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t,t^{-1}]
-module
N
, the adjoint of
\id_{j^{*}N}
under the
adjunction (
5.7
) is the

\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t,t^{-1}]
-homomorphism

(9.12)

\rho\colon j_{*}j^{*}N\to N,

which is explicitly given by

N(?)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t]}\mor_{\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t,t^{-1}]}(??,?)\to N(??)
sending

x\otimes u
to
N(u)(x)=xu
.

Lemma 9.13
.

(i)

The

\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t]
-homomorphism
\lambda\colon M\to j^{*}j_{*}M

of (
9.11
) is a localization;

(ii)

The
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}_{\phi}[t,t^{-1}]
-homomorphism

\alpha\colon j_{*}M\to S^{-1}M
of (
9.10
) is an
isomorphism, which is natural in
M
;

(iii)

Let
N
be a

\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t,t^{-1}]
-module. Then the
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t,t^{-1}]
-map

\rho\colon j_{*}j^{*}N\to N
of (
9.12
) is an isomorphism.

Proof.

(i)

Let
f\colon M\to N
be a
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t]
-map with a local

\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t]
-module as target. Because of
Lemma
9.7
there is a
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t,t^{-1}]
-module

N^{\prime}
and a
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t]
-isomorphism
u\colon N\to j^{*}N^{\prime}
. Let the

\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t,t^{-1}]
-map
v_{0}\colon j_{*}M\to N
be the adjoint of
u\circ f

under the adjunction (
5.7
). Because of the naturality
of the adjunction (
5.7
) we get for the composite

\overline{f}\colon j^{*}j_{*}M\xrightarrow{j^{*}v_{0}}j^{*}N^{\prime}\xrightarrow{u^{-1}}N
that

\overline{f}\circ\lambda=f
holds. We conclude that

\overline{f}
is uniquely determined by
\overline{f}\circ\lambda=f

from the explicite description of

\lambda
and from the fact that for any morphism
u\colon A\to B
in

\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t,t^{-1}]
there is a natural number such that the composite of
u
with

\id_{\Phi^{m}(B)}\cdot t^{m}\colon\Phi(M)\to\Phi^{m}(B)
lies in
\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t]
.

(ii)

Obviously
\alpha
is natural in
M
. The naturality of the adjunction (
5.7
) implies

j^{*}\alpha\circ\lambda=I.

Since both
I\colon M\to j^{*}S^{-1}M
and

\lambda\colon M\to j^{*}j_{*}M
a localizations,

j^{*}\alpha
and hence
\alpha
are bijective.

(iii)
It suffices to show that

j^{*}\rho\colon j^{*}j_{*}j^{*}N\to j^{*}N
is bijective.
Assertion
(i)
applied to
j^{*}N
and the
naturality of the adjunction (
5.7
) imply that

j^{*}\rho\colon j^{*}N\to j^{*}j_{*}j^{*}N
is a localization. Since

\id_{j^{*}N}\colon j^{*}N\to j^{*}N
is a localization,
j^{*}\rho
is an isomorphism.
∎

Lemma 9.14
.

The functor
j_{*}\colon\matheurm{MOD}\text{-}\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}_{\phi}[t]\to\matheurm{MOD}\text{-}\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}_{\phi}[t,t^{-1}]
is flat.

Proof.

Because of the adjunction (
5.7
) the functor
j_{*}
is
right exact by a general argument, see
[
25
, Theorem 2.6.1. on page 51]
.
Hence it remains to show that for an injective
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}_{\phi}[t]
-map
i\colon M\to N

the
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}_{\phi}[t,t^{-1}]
-map
j_{*}i\colon j_{*}M\to j_{*}N
is injective. In view of
Lemma
9.13

(ii)

it suffices to show that
S^{-1}i\colon S^{-1}M\to S^{-1}N
is injective.
Consider an object
A
in
\mathcal{A}
and an element
[l,x]
in the kernel of
S^{-1}(i)(A)
. Since

S^{-1}i([l,x])=[l,i(\Phi^{l}(A))(x)]
, there is a natural number
m\leq l

such that
N(\id_{\Phi^{l}(A)}\cdot t^{m-l})(i(\Phi^{l}(A))(x))=0
.
Since
N(\id_{\Phi^{l}(A)}\cdot t^{m-l})\circ i(\Phi^{l}(A))=i(\Phi^{m-l}(A))\circ M(\id_{\Phi^{l}(A)}\cdot t^{m-l})

and
i(\Phi^{m-l}(A))
is by assumption injective,
M(\id_{\Phi^{l}(A)}\cdot t^{m-l})(x)=0
.
This implies
[l,x]=0
.
∎

9.
c
.
Global dimension

Recall that an additive category
\mathcal{A}
has
global dimension
\leq d
, if
the abelian category
\matheurm{MOD}\text{-}\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}
has global dimension
\leq d
, i.e.,
if every
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}
-module has a projective
d
-dimensional resolution,
see Definition
6.2

(vi)
.

Theorem 9.15

(Global dimension and the passage from
\mathcal{A}
to
\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}{[t]}
)
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an additive category
\mathcal{A}
and
\Phi\colon\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}
be an
automorphism of additive categories.

(i)

Let
M
be a

\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t]
-module. If
\pdim_{\mathcal{A}}(i^{*}M)\leq d
, then
\pdim_{\mathcal{A}[t]}(M)\leq d+1
;

(ii)

If
\mathcal{A}
has global dimension
\leq d
, then
\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t]
has global dimension
\leq(d+1)
.

Theorem
9.15
is a version
of the Hilbert syzygy theorem. Its the proof is not much
different from the classical syzygy Theorem for rings. For a more general version
see
[
15
, Corollary 31.1 on page 119]
.

Proof of Theorem
9.15
.

Obviously
i^{*}\colon\matheurm{MOD}\text{-}\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t]\to\matheurm{MOD}\text{-}\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}

is faithfully flat and is compatible with direct sums over arbitrary index sets. Next we show that

i^{*}
sends projective
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}_{\phi}[t]
-modules to projective

\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}
-modules. It suffices to show that

i^{*}\mor_{\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t]}(?,A)\cong i^{*}i_{*}\mor_{\mathcal{A}}(?,A)
is free as a
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}
-module for any object

A
. This follows from the
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}
-isomorphism (
9.5
),
since
(\Phi^{k})^{*}\mor_{\mathcal{A}}(?,A)\cong\mor_{\mathcal{A}}(?,\Phi^{-k}(A))
.

The functor
i_{*}\colon\matheurm{MOD}\text{-}\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}\to\matheurm{MOD}\text{-}\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t]
is compatible
with direct sums over arbitrary index sets, is right exact and sends
\mor_{\mathcal{A}}(?,A)

to
\mor_{\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t]}(?,A)
. In particular
i_{*}
respects the properties finitely
generated, free, and projective. Next we want to show that
i_{*}
is faithfully flat. For
this purpose it suffices to show that
i^{*}\circ i_{*}
is faithfully flat. This is obvious
since
i^{*}\circ i_{*}
is the functor sending a morphism
f\colon M\to N
to the morphism

\bigoplus_{k\in\mathbb{N}}(\Phi^{k})^{*}(f)\colon\bigoplus_{k\in\mathbb{N}}(\Phi^{k})^{*}(M)\to\bigoplus_{k\in\mathbb{N}}(\Phi^{k})^{*}(f)
under the identification (
9.5
).

Now consider a
\mathbb{Z}_{\Phi}[t]
-module
M
with
\pdim_{\mathcal{A}}(i^{*}M)\leq d
.
Since the
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}
-modules
i^{*}M
and
\Phi^{*}i^{*}M
are isomorphic, see (
9.1
),
we get
\pdim_{\mathcal{A}}(\phi^{*}i^{*}M)\leq d
. Since
i_{*}
is faithfully flat and
respects projective modules, we conclude
\pdim_{\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t]}(i_{*}i^{*}M)\leq d
and

\pdim_{\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t]}(i_{*}\Phi^{*}i^{*}M)\leq d
. Now
Lemma
5.4

(v)
and
Lemma
9.3
together imply
\pdim_{\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t]}(M)\leq(d+1)
.

(ii)

This follows directly from assertion
(i)
.
∎

Theorem 9.16

(Global dimension and the passage from
\mathcal{A}_{\phi}{[t]}
to
\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}{[t,t^{-1}]}
)
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an additive category and let
\Phi\colon\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}
be an
automorphism of additive categories.

(i)

Let
M
be a
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t,t^{-1}]
-module.
If we have
\pdim_{\mathcal{A}[t]}(j^{*}M)\leq d
, then we get
\pdim_{\mathcal{A}[t,t^{-1}]}(M)\leq d
;

(ii)

If

\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t]
has global dimension
\leq d
, then
\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t,t^{-1}]
has global
dimension
\leq d
.

Proof.

(i)

Let
M
be a
\mathbb{Z}[\mathcal{A}]_{\Phi}[t,t^{-1}]
-module satisfying
\pdim_{\mathcal{A}[t]}(j^{*}M)\leq d
. The
functor
j_{*}\colon\matheurm{MOD}\text{-}\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}_{\phi}[t]\to\matheurm{MOD}\text{-}\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}_{\phi}[t,t^{-1}]

is flat by Lemma
9.14
. Since it respects the property projective,
we get
\pdim_{\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t,t^{-1}]}(j_{*}j^{*}M)\leq d
.
Lemma
9.13

(iii)
implies

\pdim_{\mathcal{A}[t,t^{-1}]}(M)\leq d
.

(ii)

This follows from assertion
(i)
.
∎

10.
Regular additive categories

Regularity for additive categories
\mathcal{A}
requires finite resolutions of finitely
presented modules, but not for arbitrary modules. In particular, regularity has no
consequence for global dimension and we cannot use
Theorem
9.15
in the following result.

Theorem 10.1

(Regularity and the passage from
\mathcal{A}
to
\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}{[t]}
)
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an additive category
\mathcal{A}
and let
\Phi\colon\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}
be an
automorphism of additive categories. Let
l
be a natural number.

(i)

Suppose that
\mathcal{A}
is regular or
l
-uniformly regular respectively.
Then
\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t]
is regular or
(l+2)
-uniformly regular respectively;

(ii)

Suppose that
\mathcal{A}[t]
is regular or
l
-uniformly regular respectively.
Then
\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t,t^{-1}]
is regular or
l
-uniformly regular respectively.

Proof.

(i)
We know already that

\mathcal{A}_{\phi[t]}
is Noetherian because of
Theorem
8.1

(i)
.
Let
M
be a finitely generated
\mathcal{A}_{\phi}[t]
-module. We have to show that it has a
finitely generated projective resolution, which is finite-dimensional or

(l+1)
-dimensional. Since
\mathcal{A}_{\phi}[t]
is Noetherian, there exists a finitely
generated projective resolution of
M
, which may be infinite-dimensional. We conclude
from Theorem
5.4

(iv)
that
it suffices to show the projective dimension of
M
is finite or bounded by
(l+1)

respectively. As
M
is finitely generated, we find a finite collection of elements

x_{j}\in M(Z_{j})
with objects
Z_{j}
from
\mathcal{A}
such that the
x_{j}
generate
M
as an

\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t]
-module. For
d\geq 0
consider the morphism

\id_{Z_{j}}\cdot t^{d}\colon\Phi^{-d}(Z_{j})\to Z_{j}
in
\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t]
and set

x_{j}[d]:=M(\id_{Z_{j}}\cdot t^{d})(x_{j})\in M(\Phi^{-d}(Z_{j}))
. Let
M_{n}
be the

\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}
-submodule of
i^{*}M
generated by all
x_{j}[d]
with
d\leq n
. We obtain an
increasing subsequence
M_{0}\subseteq M_{0}\subseteq M_{1}\subseteq M_{2}\subseteq\cdots

of
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}
-submodules of
i^{*}M
with
i^{*}M=\bigcup_{n\geq 0}M_{n}
. Let

T_{n}\colon i^{*}M\to\Phi^{*}i^{*}M
be the following
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}
-morphism. For an object

Z
from
\mathcal{A}
consider

\id_{\Phi^{n}(Z)}\cdot t^{n}\in\mor_{\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t]}(Z,\Phi^{n}(Z))
and define

T_{Z}\colon i^{*}M(Z)=M(Z)\to\Phi^{*}i^{*}M(Z)=M(\Phi(Z))
to be

M(\id_{\Phi^{n}(Z)}\cdot t^{n})
. Let

\pr_{n}\colon(\Phi^{n})^{*}(M_{n})\to(\Phi^{n})^{*}(M_{n})/(\Phi^{n})^{*}(M_{n-1})
be the
projection. The composite

f_{n}\colon M_{0}\xrightarrow{\overline{T_{n}}}(\Phi^{n})^{*}(M_{n})\xrightarrow{\pr_{n}}(\Phi^{n})^{*}(M_{n})/(\Phi^{n})^{*}(M_{n-1})

is surjective and we write
K_{n}
for its kernel. We obtain an increasing sequence of

\mathcal{A}
-submodules
K_{0}\subseteq K_{1}\subseteq K_{2}\subseteq\cdots
of
M_{0}
. Since

\mathcal{A}
is Noetherian and
M_{0}
is finitely generated, there exists an integer

n_{0}\geq 1
such that
K_{n}=K_{n_{0}}
holds for
n\geq n_{0}
. Define

g_{n}\colon(\Phi^{n_{0}})^{*}M_{n_{0}}/(\Phi^{n_{0}})^{*}M_{n_{0}-1}\to(\Phi^{n})^{*}M_{n}/(\Phi^{n})^{*}M_{n-1}
for
n\geq n_{0}
to be the map induced by

\Phi^{*}({T_{n-n_{0}}})
for
n\geq n_{0}
. We obtain for every natural number
n
with

n\geq n_{0}
a commutative diagram of
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}
-modules with exact rows

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K_{n_{0}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{M_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{n_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\id_{M_{0}}}
\textstyle{(\Phi^{n_{0}})^{*}M_{n_{0}}/(\Phi^{n_{0}})^{*}M_{n_{0}-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g_{n}}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{n}}
\textstyle{(\Phi^{n})^{*}M_{n}/(\Phi^{n})^{*}M_{n-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

Hence
g_{n}
is an isomorphism of
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}
-module
n\geq n_{0}
.
As
\Phi^{*}
is an isomorphism we have

\pdim_{\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}}M_{n}/M_{n-1}=\pdim_{\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}}(\Phi^{n})^{*}(M_{n}/M_{n-1})=\pdim_{\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}}(\Phi^{n})^{*}M_{n}/(\Phi^{n})^{*}M_{n-1}).

Thus for
n\geq n_{0}
we have
\pdim_{\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}}(M_{n}/M_{n-1})=\pdim_{\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}}(M_{n_{0}}/M_{n_{0}-1})
.
We have the short exact sequence
0\to M_{n-1}\to M_{n}\to M_{n}/M_{n-1}\to 0
and hence get from
Lemma
5.4

(v)

\pdim_{\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}}(M_{n})\leq\sup\{\pdim_{\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}}(M_{n-1}),\pdim_{\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}}(M_{n}/M_{n-1})\}.

This implies by induction over
n\geq n_{0}

\pdim_{\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}}(M_{n})\leq\sup\{\pdim_{\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}}(M_{n_{0}-1}),\pdim_{\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}}(M_{n_{0}}/M_{n_{0}-1})\}.

Put

D:=\sup\bigl\{\sup\{\pdim_{\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}}(M_{k})\mid k=0,1,\ldots,n_{0}-1\},\pdim_{\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}}(M_{n_{0}}/M_{n_{0}-1})\bigr\}.

Note that
D<\infty
, if
\mathcal{A}
is regular, and
D\leq l
, if
\mathcal{A}
is uniformly
l
-regular.
We get

\pdim_{\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}}\biggl(\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb{N}}M_{n}\biggr)\leq\sup\{\pdim_{\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}}(M_{n})\mid n\geq 0\}\leq D.

We have the short exact sequence of
\mathcal{A}
-modules

0\to\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb{N}}M_{n}\to\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb{N}}M_{n}\to i^{*}M\to 0,

where the first map is given by
(x_{n})_{n\geq 0}\mapsto(x_{0},x_{1}-x_{0}.x_{2},-x_{1},\ldots)

and the second by
(x_{n})_{n\geq 0}\mapsto\sum_{n\geq 0}x_{n}
. We conclude from
Lemma
5.4

(v)

\pdim_{\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}}(i^{*}M)\leq D+1.

Now Theorem
9.15

(i)

implies

\pdim_{\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t]}(M)\leq D+2.

This finishes the proof if assertion
(i)
.

(ii)

We know already that

\mathcal{A}_{\phi}[t,t^{-1}]
is Noetherian because of
Theorem
8.1

(ii)
.
Let
M
be a finitely generated
\mathcal{A}_{\phi}[t,t^{-1}]
-module. We can find a
finitely generated free
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t]
-module
F_{0}
and a free

\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t]
-module
F_{1}
together with an exact sequence of

\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t,t^{-1}]
-modules

j_{*}\F_{0}\xrightarrow{f}j_{*}F_{1}\xrightarrow{e}M\to 0
. Here we write
j
for the
inclusion
\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t]\to\mathcal{A}_{\phi}[t,t^{-1}]
. By composing
f
with an
appropriate automorphism of
j_{*}F_{0}
one can arrange that
f=j_{*}g
for some

\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t]
-homomorphism
g\colon F_{0}\to F_{1}
. The cokernel of
g
is a
finitely generated
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t]
-module
N
and there is an obvious exact
sequence of
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t]
-modules
F_{1}\xrightarrow{g}F_{1}\to N\to 0
. Since
the functor
j_{*}
is flat by Lemma
9.14
and respects the property
projective, we obtain an
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t,t^{-1}]
-isomorphism

j_{*}N\xrightarrow{\cong}M
and have

\dim_{\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t,t^{-1}]}(j_{*}N)\leq\dim_{\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t]}(N)
. Hence we
get
\dim_{\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t,t^{-1}]}(M)\leq\dim_{\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}[t]}(N)
. This
finishes the proof of Theorem
10.1
.
∎

Remark 10.2
.

We do not know whether Theorem
10.1

remains true if we replace regular by regular coherent. To our knowledge it is an open problem,
whether for a regular coherent ring
R
the rings
R[t]
or
R[t,t^{-1}]
are regular coherent again.

11.
Directed union and infinite products of additive categories

A functor of additive categories
F\colon\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
is called
flat
, if for
every exact sequence
A_{0}\xrightarrow{i}A_{1}\xrightarrow{p}A_{2}
in
\mathcal{A}
the sequence
in
F(A_{0})\xrightarrow{F(i)}F(A_{1})\xrightarrow{F(p)}F(A_{2})
in
\mathcal{B}
is exact.
It is called
faithfully flat
, if
a sequence
A_{0}\xrightarrow{i}A_{1}\xrightarrow{p}A_{2}
in
\mathcal{A}
is exact, if and only if the sequence
in
F(A_{0})\xrightarrow{F(i)}F(A_{1})\xrightarrow{F(p)}F(A_{2})
in
\mathcal{B}
is exact.

Lemma 11.1
.

Let
i\colon\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
and
j\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
be inclusions of cofinal
full additive subcategories. Suppose that the following diagram of functors of additive
categories commutes

\textstyle{\mathcal{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{\mathcal{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}

Then

(i)

The inclusion
i\colon\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
is faithfully flat;

(ii)

F
is flat or faithfully flat respectively if and only if
F^{\prime}
is flat or faithfully flat respectively.

Proof.

We first show that
F^{\prime}
is exact or
faithfully exact respectively, provided that
F
is exact or faithfully exact respectively.

Consider morphisms
f^{\prime}\colon A^{\prime}_{0}\to A^{\prime}_{1}
and
g^{\prime}\colon A_{1}^{\prime}\to A_{2}^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{A}
.
Choose objects
A_{k}
in
\mathcal{A}
and morphisms
i_{k}\colon A_{k}^{\prime}\to A_{k}
and

r_{k}\colon A_{k}\to A_{k}^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
satisfying
r_{k}\circ i_{k}=\id_{A_{k}}
for

k=0,1,2
. Define
f\colon A_{0}\to A_{1}
and
g\colon A_{1}\to A_{2}
by

f=i_{1}\circ f^{\prime}\circ r_{0}
and
g=i_{2}\circ g^{\prime}\circ r_{1}
. Then the following diagram of
morphisms in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
commutes

\textstyle{A_{0}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{i_{0}}
\textstyle{A_{1}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{i_{1}}
\textstyle{A_{2}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{2}\oplus 0}
\textstyle{A_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{r_{0}}
\textstyle{A_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g\oplus(\id_{A_{1}}-i_{1}\circ r_{1})}
\scriptstyle{r_{1}}
\textstyle{A_{2}\oplus A_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{r_{2}\oplus 0}
\textstyle{A_{0}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime}}
\textstyle{A_{1}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g^{\prime}}
\textstyle{A_{2}^{\prime}.}

Next we check that the middle row is exact in
\mathcal{A}
, if and only if the upper row is
exact in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
. Suppose that the middle row is exact in
\mathcal{A}
. Consider a morphism

v^{\prime}\colon B^{\prime}\to A_{1}^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
such that
g^{\prime}\circ v^{\prime}=0
. Choose an object
B
in
\mathcal{A}

and maps
j\colon B^{\prime}\to B
and
s\colon B\to B^{\prime}
with
s\circ j=\id_{B^{\prime}}
. Then
we have the morphism
i_{1}\circ v^{\prime}\circ s\colon B\to A_{1}
whose composite with

g\oplus(\id_{A_{1}}-i_{1}\circ r_{1})\colon A_{1}\to A_{2}\oplus A_{1}
is zero. Hence we can find a
morphism
u_{0}\colon B\to A_{0}
with
f\circ u_{0}=i_{1}\circ v^{\prime}\circ s
. Define

u^{\prime}\colon B^{\prime}\to A_{0}^{\prime}
by the composite
r_{0}\circ u_{0}\circ j
. One easily checks
that
f^{\prime}\circ u^{\prime}=v^{\prime}
. Hence the upper row is exact in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
.

Suppose that the upper row is exact in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
. Consider a morphism
v\colon B\to A_{1}
in
\mathcal{A}

such that
g\oplus(\id_{A_{1}}-i_{1}\circ r_{1})\circ v=0
. Then
g\circ v=0
and

v=i_{1}\circ r_{1}\circ v
. We conclude

g^{\prime}\circ(r_{1}\circ v)=r_{2}\circ i_{2}\circ g^{\prime}\circ r_{1}\circ v=r_{2}\circ g\circ i_{1}\circ r_{1}\circ v=r_{2}\circ g\circ v=r_{2}\circ 0=0.

Since the upper row is exact, we can find
u^{\prime}\colon B\to A_{0}^{\prime}
satisfying
f^{\prime}\circ u_{0}^{\prime}=r_{1}\circ v
.
Define
u\colon B\to A_{0}
by
i_{0}\circ u^{\prime}
. Then

f\circ u=f\circ i_{0}\circ u^{\prime}=i_{1}\circ f^{\prime}\circ u^{\prime}=i_{1}\circ r_{1}\circ v=v.

Hence the middle row is exact.

If we apply
F^{\prime}
and put
i_{k}^{\prime}=F^{\prime}(i_{k})
and
r_{k}^{\prime}=F^{\prime}(r_{k})
,
we get
r_{k}^{\prime}\circ i_{k}^{\prime}=\id_{F^{\prime}(A_{k}^{\prime})}
and the commutative diagram

\textstyle{F^{\prime}(A_{0}^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F^{\prime}(f^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{i_{0}^{\prime}}
\textstyle{F^{\prime}(A_{1}^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F^{\prime}(g^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{i_{1}^{\prime}}
\textstyle{F^{\prime}(A_{2}^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{2}^{\prime}\oplus 0}
\textstyle{F(A_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F(f)}
\scriptstyle{r_{0}^{\prime}}
\textstyle{F(A_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F(g)\oplus(\id_{F(A_{1})}-i_{1}^{\prime}\circ r_{1}^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{r_{1}^{\prime}}
\textstyle{F^{\prime}(A_{2})\oplus F(A_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{r_{2}\oplus 0}
\textstyle{F^{\prime}(A_{0}^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F^{\prime}(f^{\prime})}
\textstyle{F^{\prime}(A_{1}^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F^{\prime}(g^{\prime})}
\textstyle{F^{\prime}(A_{2}^{\prime})}

and, by the same argument as above,
the middle row is exact in
\mathcal{B}
, if and only if the upper row is exact in
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
.
We conclude that the functor
F^{\prime}
is exact or
faithfully exact respectively, provided that
F
is exact or faithfully exact respectively.

Since both
\id_{\mathcal{A}}
and
\id_{\mathcal{B}}
are faithfully flat, this special case shows
that both
i\colon\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
and
j\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
are faithfully
flat.

Suppose that
F^{\prime}
is flat or faithfully flat respectively. Then

j\circ F=F^{\prime}\circ i
is flat or faithfully flat respectively. This implies that

F
is flat or faithfully flat respectively. This finishes the proof of
Lemma
11.1
.
∎

Lemma 11.2
.

Let
\mathcal{A}=\bigcup_{i\in I}\mathcal{A}_{i}
be the directed union of
additive subcategories
\mathcal{A}_{i}
for an arbitrary directed set
I
.

(i)

The idempotent
completion
\Idem(\mathcal{A})
is the directed union of the idempotent completions

\Idem(\mathcal{A}_{i})
;

(ii)

Consider
l\geq 1
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{A}_{i}
is regular coherent or
l
-uniformly regular
coherent respectively for every
i\in I
and for every
i,j\in I
with

i\leq j
the inclusion
\mathcal{A}_{i}\to\mathcal{A}_{j}
is flat.
Then the inclusion
\Idem(\mathcal{A}_{i})\to\Idem(\mathcal{A}_{j})
is flat for every
i,j\in I
with

i\leq j
and both
\mathcal{A}
and
\Idem(\mathcal{A})
are regular coherent or
l
-uniformly regular coherent respectively;

(iii)

Suppose that
\mathcal{A}_{i}
is
0
-uniformly regular
coherent respectively for every
i\in I
.
Then both
\mathcal{A}
and
\Idem(\mathcal{A})
are
0
-uniformly regular coherent respectively.

Proof.

(i)

This is obvious.

(ii)
If
the inclusion
\mathcal{A}_{i}\to\mathcal{A}_{j}
is flat, then also the
inclusion
\Idem(\mathcal{A}_{i})\to\Idem(\mathcal{A}_{j})
is flat by Lemma
11.1
.
In view of Lemma
6.4

(vi)

and assertion
(i)
,
we can assume without loss of generality
that each
\mathcal{A}_{i}
and
\mathcal{A}
are idempotent complete.
Hence we can use the criterion for regular coherent given in
Lemma
6.6
in the sequel. We treat only the case
l\geq 2
,
the case
l=1
is proved analogously

Consider a morphism
f_{1}\colon A_{1}\to A_{0}
in
\mathcal{A}
. Choose an index
i
such that

f_{1}
belongs to
\mathcal{A}_{i}
. Then we can find a sequence of morphisms

0\to A_{n}\xrightarrow{f_{n}}A_{n-1}\xrightarrow{f_{n-1}}\cdots\xrightarrow{f_{2}}A_{1}\xrightarrow{f_{1}}A_{0}

which is in
\mathcal{A}_{i}
exact at
A_{k}
for
k=1,2,\ldots,n
. It remains to show that
this sequence is exact at
\mathcal{A}
at
A_{k}
for
k=1,2,\ldots,n
. Fix

k\in\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
. It remains to show for any object
A\in\mathcal{A}
and morphism

g\colon A\to A_{k}
with
f_{k}\circ g=0
that there exists a morphism

\overline{g}\colon A\to A_{k+1}
with
f_{k+1}\circ\overline{g}=g
. We can choose

j\in I
with
i\leq j
such that
g
belongs to
\mathcal{A}_{j}
. Since

A_{k+1}\xrightarrow{f_{k+1}}A_{k}\xrightarrow{f_{k}}A_{k-1}
is exact in
\mathcal{A}_{i}
,
we conclude from the assumptions that it is also exact in
\mathcal{A}_{j}
and hence we can
construct the desired lift
\overline{g}
already in
\mathcal{A}_{j}
.

(iii)

In view of Lemma
6.4

(vi)

and assertion
(i)
,
we can assume without loss of generality
that each
\mathcal{A}_{i}
and
\mathcal{A}
are idempotent complete.
Now the claim follows from the
equivalence
(iii)a

\Longleftrightarrow

(iii)c

appearing in Lemma
6.6

(iii)
.
∎

Lemma 11.3
.

Let
l
be a natural number. Let
\mathcal{A}=\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\mid i\in I\}
be a
collection of additive categories

\mathcal{A}_{i}
for an arbitrary index set
I
. Let
l
be a natural number.

(i)

Suppose that each
\mathcal{A}_{i}
is

l
-uniformly regular coherent. Then
\prod_{i\in I}\Idem(\mathcal{A}_{i})
is

l
-uniformly regular coherent;

(ii)

Suppose that each
\mathcal{A}_{i}
is

l
-uniformly regular coherent,
l
-uniformly regular, regular coherent, regular, or Noetherian.
Then
\bigoplus_{i\in I}\mathcal{A}_{i}
has the same property.

Proof.

Obviously
\prod_{i\in I}\mathcal{A}_{i}
inherits the structure of an additive category.
Recall that
\bigoplus_{i\in I}\mathcal{A}_{i}
is the full additive subcategory of

\prod_{i\in I}\mathcal{A}_{i}
consisting of those objects
A_{i}\mid i\in I\}
, for which only
finitely many of the objects
A_{i}
are different from zero. Obviously

\displaystyle\Idem(\bigoplus_{i\in I}\mathcal{A}_{i})

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{i\in I}\Idem(\mathcal{A}_{i});

\displaystyle\Idem(\prod_{i\in I}\mathcal{A}_{i})

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\prod_{i\in I}\Idem(\mathcal{A}_{i}).

Lemma
6.6
implies
that
\bigoplus_{i\in I}\Idem(\mathcal{A}_{i})
and
\prod_{i\in I}\Idem(\mathcal{A}_{i})
are
l
-uniformly
regular coherent, if each
\Idem(\mathcal{A}_{i})
is
l
-uniformly
regular coherent. We conclude from Lemma
6.4

(vi)

that
\bigoplus_{i\in I}\mathcal{A}_{i}
and
\prod_{i\in I}\mathcal{A}_{i}
are
l
-uniformly
regular coherent, if each
\mathcal{A}_{i}
is
l
-uniformly regular coherent.

Analogously one shows that
\bigoplus_{i\in I}\mathcal{A}_{i}
is regular coherent
if each
\mathcal{A}_{i}
is regular coherent.

Next we show that
\bigoplus_{i\in I}\mathcal{A}_{i}
is Noetherian
if each
\mathcal{A}_{i}
is Noetherian.
Consider any object
A
in
\bigoplus_{i\in I}\mathcal{A}_{i}
. Choose a finite index set

J\subseteq I
such that
A
belongs to
\bigoplus_{i\in J}\mathcal{A}_{i}
. Let

\pr\colon\bigoplus_{i\in I}\mathcal{A}_{i}\to\bigoplus_{i\in J}\mathcal{A}_{i}
be the
projection. Consider two morphism
f_{0}\colon A_{0}\to A
and
f_{1}\colon A_{1}\to A
in

\bigoplus_{i\in I}\mathcal{A}_{i}
. Then
f_{0}=f_{1}
holds if and only if
\pr(f_{0})=\pr(f_{1})

holds. Hence
f_{0}\subseteq f_{1}
holds in
\bigoplus_{i\in I}\mathcal{A}_{i}
if and only if

\pr(f_{0})\subseteq\pr(f_{1})
holds in
\bigoplus_{i\in J}\mathcal{A}_{i}
. We conclude from
Lemma
6.8
that it suffices to show that

\bigoplus_{i\in J}\mathcal{A}_{i}
is Noetherian for any finite subset
J\subseteq I
. But
this is an easy consequence of Lemma
6.8

again. ∎

Lemma
11.3

(i)

will be generalized in Lemma
13.10
.

Remark 11.4

(Advantage of the notion
l
-uniformly regular coherent)
.

The decisive advantage of the notion
l
-uniformly regular coherent is that it satisfies
both Lemma
11.2
and
Lemma
11.3
.
Lemma
11.2
and
Lemma
11.3

(i)

are not true, if one replaces
l
-uniformly regular coherent by any of the properties
regular coherent,
l
-uniformly regular, regular or Noetherian, unless
I
is finite.

12.
Vanishing of negative
K
-groups

Theorem 12.1

(Vanishing of negative
K
-groups)
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an additive category, such that
\mathcal{A}[t_{1},t_{2},\ldots,t_{m}]
is regular
coherent for every
m\geq 0
.

Then
K_{n}(\mathcal{A})=0
holds for all
n\leq-1
.

Proof.

For an additive category
\mathcal{B}
define
G^{\prime}_{0}(\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{B})
to be the abelian group with
isomorphism classes
[M]
of finitely presented
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{B}
-modules
M
as generators
such that for each exact sequence of finitely presented
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{B}
-modules

0\to M_{0}\to M_{1}\to M_{2}\to 0
we have the relation
[M_{0}]-[M_{1}]+[M_{2}]=0
. Define
K_{0}(\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{B})
analogously but with finitely presented replaced by finitely
generated projective. A functor of additive categories
F\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B}^{\prime}

induces a homomorphism
F_{*}\colon K_{0}(\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{B})\to K_{0}(\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{B}^{\prime})
by sending
[M]
to

[F_{*}M]
. It induces a homomorphism
F_{*}\colon G^{\prime}_{0}(\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{B})\to G^{\prime}_{0}(\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{B}^{\prime})
by
sending
[M]
to
[F_{*}M]
, if

F_{*}\colon\matheurm{MOD}\text{-}\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{B}}\to\matheurm{MOD}\text{-}\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}
is flat. There
is the forgetful functor
U\colon K_{0}(\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{B})\to G^{\prime}_{0}(\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{B}^{\prime})
. If
\mathcal{B}
is
regular coherent, then
U
is a bijection by the Resolution Theorem,
see
[
20
, Theorem 4.6 on page 41]
. The Yoneda embedding induces an
isomorphism
K_{0}(\mathcal{B})\xrightarrow{\cong}K_{0}(\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{B})
, natural in
\mathcal{B}
.

Suppose
\mathcal{A}[t]
is regular coherent. We show that
\mathcal{A}[t,t^{-1}]
is regular
coherent and
K_{-1}(\mathcal{A})=0
. The functor

j_{*}\colon\matheurm{MOD}\text{-}\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}[t]\to\matheurm{MOD}\text{-}\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}{[t,t^{-1}]}
is flat by
Lemma
9.14
. Let
M_{*}
be a finitely presented

\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}[t,t^{-1}]
-module. Then we can find a morphism
f\colon A\to A^{\prime}
in

\mathcal{A}[t,t^{-1}]
together with an exact sequence of
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}[t,t^{1-}]
-modules

\mor_{\mathcal{A}[t,t^{-1}]}(?,A)\xrightarrow{f_{*}}\mor_{\mathcal{A}[t,t^{-1}]}(?,A^{\prime})\to M\to 0.

Choose a natural number
s
and a morphism
g\colon A\to A^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{A}[t]
such that

(\id_{A^{\prime}}\cdot t^{s})\circ f=g
holds in
\mathcal{A}[t,t^{-1}]
. Since

\id_{A^{\prime}}\cdot t^{s}\colon A^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\cong}A^{\prime}
is an isomorphism in

\mathcal{A}[t,t^{-1}]
, we obtain an exact sequence of
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}[t,t^{-1}]
-modules

j_{*}(\mor_{\mathcal{A}[t]}(?,A))\xrightarrow{j_{*}(g_{*})}j_{*}(\mor_{\mathcal{A}[t]}(?,A^{\prime}))\to M\to 0.

Let
N
be the finitely presented
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}[t]
-module, which is the cokernel of the

\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}[t]
-homomorphism
g_{*}\colon\mor_{\mathcal{A}[t]}(?,A)\to\mor_{\mathcal{A}[t]}(?,A^{\prime})
.
Since
j_{*}
is flat and in particular right exact, we obtain an isomorphism of finitely
presented
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}[t,t^{-1}]
-modules
j_{*}N\xrightarrow{\cong}M
. This implies that
the homomorphism
j_{*}\colon G^{\prime}_{0}(\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}[t])\to G^{\prime}_{0}(\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}[t,t^{-1}])
is
surjective and that
\mathcal{A}[t,t^{-1}]
is regular coherent since
\mathcal{A}[t]
is regular
coherent by assumption.

Hence we obtain a commutative diagram

\textstyle{K_{0}(\mathcal{A}[t])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{K_{0}(\mathcal{A}[t,t^{-1}])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{K_{0}(\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}[t])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{K_{0}(\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}[t,t^{-1}])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{G^{\prime}_{0}(\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}[t])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{G^{\prime}_{0}(\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}[t,t^{-1}]),}

whose vertical arrows are bijections and whose lowermost horizontal arrow is surjective.
Hence the uppermost horizontal arrow is surjective. We conclude from
Theorem
4.2
that
K_{-1}(\mathcal{A})
vanishes.

Next we show by induction for
n=1,2\ldots
that
K_{-m}(\mathcal{A})
vanishes for

m=1,2,\ldots,n
. The induction beginning
n=1
has been taken care of above. The
induction step from
n\geq 1
to
n+1
is done as follows. One shows using the claim
above by induction for
i=1,2,\ldots,n
that
\mathcal{A}[\mathbb{Z}^{i}][t_{i+1},\dots,t_{n+1}]

is regular coherent. In particular
\mathcal{A}[\mathbb{Z}^{n}][t_{n+1}]
is regular coherent.

We conclude from the
n
-times iterated Bass-Heller-Swan isomorphism, see
Theorem
4.1
that
K_{-n-1}(\mathcal{A})

is a direct summand in
K_{-1}(\mathcal{A}[\mathbb{Z}^{n}])
. Hence it suffices to show that

K_{-1}(\mathcal{A}[\mathbb{Z}^{n}])
is trivial. This follows from the induction beginning applied to

\mathcal{A}[\mathbb{Z}^{n}]
.
∎

We conclude from Theorem
10.1

and Theorem
12.1

Corollary 12.2

(Vanishing of negative
K
-groups of regular additive categories)
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an additive category which is regular.

Then
K_{n}(\mathcal{A})=0
holds for all
n\leq-1
.

Remark 12.3
.

As noted in Lemma
5.10
the Yoneda embedding identifies
\mathcal{A}

with the category of finitely generated free
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}
-modules. If
\mathcal{A}
is
Noetherian, then the category of finitely generated
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{A}
-modules is abelian. If
it is in addition regular coherent (i.e., if
\mathcal{A}
is regular), then
\mathcal{A}
is
derived equivalent to this abelian category. Schlichting showed in
[
19
, Theorem 6 on
page 117]
that
K_{-1}
of abelian categories is trivial. It follows
that for regular
\mathcal{A}
we can obtain Theorem
12.1

from Schlichting’s result. Similarly,
Corollary
12.2
can
alternatively be deduced from
[
19
, Theorem 7 on page 118]
.

13.
Nested sequences, the associated categories, and their
K
-theory

13.
a
.
Nested sequences and the associated categories

Definition 13.1

(Nested sequences of additive categories)
.

A
nested sequence of additive categories

\mathcal{A}_{*}
is a decreasing
sequence of additive subcategories

\mathcal{A}_{0}\supseteq\mathcal{A}_{1}\supseteq\mathcal{A}_{2}\supseteq\cdots.

We have two notions of morphisms.

Definition 13.2

((Pro-)morphisms of nested sequences of additive categories)
.

A
morphism of nested sequences of additive categories

F\colon\mathcal{A}_{*}\to\mathcal{A}_{*}^{\prime}
is a sequence of functors of additive categories

F_{m}\colon\mathcal{A}_{m}\to\mathcal{A}_{m}^{\prime}
for
m\in\mathbb{N}
such that
F_{m}
restricted to

\mathcal{A}_{m+1}
is
F_{m+1}
.

A
pro-morphism of nested sequences of additive categories

F\colon\mathcal{A}_{*}\to\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{*}

is a functor of additive categories
F\colon\mathcal{A}_{0}\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{0}
such that there is a
function
N\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
with the property that for every
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
with

m\geq N(n)
we have
F(\mathcal{A}_{m})\subseteq\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{n}
.

Obviously a morphism
F_{*}
defines a pro-morphisms by taking
F_{0}
, but
not every pro-morphism comes from a morphism in this way. The
composite of two morphisms and of two pro-morphisms is defined in
the obvious way.

Note that a pro-automorphism
\phi\colon\mathcal{A}_{*}\to\mathcal{A}_{*}
is the same as an
automorphism of additive categories
\Phi\colon\mathcal{A}_{0}\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathcal{A}_{0}
such
that there is a function
N\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
with the property that for every

m,n\in\mathbb{N}
with
m\geq N(n)
we have
\Phi(\mathcal{A}_{m})\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{n}
and

\mathcal{A}_{m}\subseteq\Phi(\mathcal{A}_{n})
.

Definition 13.3

(The sequence category
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*})
and
the limit category
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{*})
)
.

Define the additive category
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*})
, called
sequence category
,
associated to the nested sequence of additive categories
\mathcal{A}_{*}
as follows:

•

An object in
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*})
is a sequence

\underline{A}=(A_{m})_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
of objects in
\mathcal{A}_{0}
such that
there exists a function (depending on
\underline{A}
)

L\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
with the property that
A_{m}
belongs to
\mathcal{A}_{l}
for

l,m\in\mathbb{N}
with
m\geq L(l)
;

•

A morphism
\underline{\varphi}\colon\underline{A}\to\underline{A^{\prime}}
in

\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*})
consists of a sequence of morphisms
\varphi_{m}\colon A_{m}\to A^{\prime}_{m}
in

\mathcal{A}_{0}
for
m\in\mathbb{N}
such that there exists a function
L\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
with the property
that
\varphi_{m}\colon A_{m}\to A_{m}^{\prime}
belongs to
\mathcal{A}_{l}
for
m,l\in\mathbb{N}
with

m\geq L(l)
;

•

Composition and the structure of an additive category on
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*})

comes from the corresponding structure on
\mathcal{A}_{0}
.

Let
\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{A}_{*})
be the full subcategory of
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*})
consisting of objects

\underline{A}
, for which there exists a natural number
M
(depending on
\underline{A}
)
with
A_{m}=0
for
m\geq M
.

The additive category
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{*})
, called
limit category
, is defined to be the
additive quotient category
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*})/\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{A}_{*})
. Recall that for a full additive subcategory

\mathcal{U}\subseteq\mathcal{B}
of an additive category
\mathcal{B}
the additive quotient category
\mathcal{B}/\mathcal{U}
has
the same objects as
\mathcal{B}
and that morphisms in
\mathcal{B}/\mathcal{U}
are equivalence classes of
morphisms in
\mathcal{B}
, where two morphisms
\varphi,\varphi^{\prime}
are identified, if their difference

\varphi-\varphi^{\prime}
can be factorized through an object of
\mathcal{U}
. For
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{*})
this
means that morphisms
\varphi,\varphi^{\prime}
from
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*})
are identified in
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{*})
,
if and only if
\varphi_{m}=\varphi^{\prime}_{m}
for all but finitely many
m
.

Obviously
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*})
is an additive subcategory of
\prod_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{A}_{0}
and
is equal to it, if the nested sequence is constant, i.e.,
\mathcal{A}_{0}=\mathcal{A}_{m}
for

m\in\mathbb{N}
. Obviously
\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{A}_{*})
can be identified with

\bigoplus_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{A}_{m}
. Controlled categories appear for instance in proofs of
the Farrell-Jones Conjecture. In for us important cases controlled categories correspond
to nested sequences of additive categories, where one should think of the control
condition to become sharper the larger the index
m
gets.

For the notion of a Karoubi filtration and the associated weak homotopy fibration sequence
we refer for instance to
[
3
]
,
and
[
6
, Definition 5.4]
or
[
11
, Section 12.2]
.
One easily checks

Lemma 13.4
.

The inclusion
\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{A}_{*})\subseteq\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*})
is a Karoubi filtration and we
have the weak homotopy fibration sequence

\mathbf{K}^{\infty}(\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{A}_{*}))\to\mathbf{K}^{\infty}(\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*}))\to\mathbf{K}^{\infty}(\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{*})).

Let
F\colon\mathcal{A}_{*}\to\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{*}
be a pro-morphisms. It induces functors of additive
categories

(13.5)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(F)\colon\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*})

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{*});

(13.6)

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}(F)\colon\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{A}_{*})

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{*});

(13.7)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(F)\colon\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{*})

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{*}),

as follows. We begin with
\mathcal{S}(F)
. It sends an object

\underline{A}=(A_{m})_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
to the object

\mathcal{S}(F)(\underline{A})=(F(A_{m}))_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
. We have to check that the latter
collection defines an element in
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{*})
. Recall that there is a function

L\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
with the property that
A_{m}
belongs to
\mathcal{A}_{l}
for
l,m\in\mathbb{N}

with
m\geq L(l)
and that there is a function
N\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
with the property
that for every
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
with
m\geq N(n)
we have
F(\mathcal{A}_{m})\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{n}
.
Consider the function
L\circ N\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
. For
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
with

m\geq L\circ N(n)
we conclude
A_{m}\in\mathcal{A}_{N(n)}
and hence

F(A_{m})\in F(\mathcal{A}_{N(n)})\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{n}
. Hence
(F(A_{m}))_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
is a
well-defined object in
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*})
.

Given a morphism
\underline{\varphi}\colon\underline{A}\to\underline{A^{\prime}}
in

\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*})
, we can define

\mathcal{S}(F)(\underline{\varphi})\colon\mathcal{S}(F)(\underline{A})\to\mathcal{S}(F)(\underline{A^{\prime}})
in
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{*})
by the collection

(F(\varphi_{m}))_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
. Obviously
\mathcal{S}(F^{\prime}\circ F)=\mathcal{S}(F^{\prime})\circ\mathcal{S}(F)
and

\mathcal{S}(\id_{\underline{A}})=\id_{\underline{A}}
holds. By construction
\mathcal{S}(F)

induces a functor of additive categories

\mathcal{T}(F)\colon\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{A}_{*})\to\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{*})
. By passing to the quotients we also
get a functor of additive categories
\mathcal{L}(F)\colon\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{*})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{*})
.

Note that a pro-automorphism

\Phi\colon\mathcal{A}_{*}\to\mathcal{A}_{*}
induces automorphisms of additive categories

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(\Phi)\colon\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*})

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\cong}

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*});

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}(\Phi)\colon\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{A}_{*})

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\cong}

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{A}_{*});

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\Phi)\colon\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{*})

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\cong}

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{*}).

Definition 13.8
.

We call a function
I\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}

admissible
, if it has the following properties

\begin{array}[]{rclcl}I(m)&\leq&m&&\text{for}\;m\in\mathbb{N};\\
I(m)&\leq&I(m+1)&&\text{for}\;m\in\mathbb{N};\\
\lim_{m\to\infty}I(m)&=&\infty.\end{array}

Let
\mathcal{I}
be the set of admissible functions. It becomes a directed set by defining for
I,J\in\mathcal{I}

I\leq J\Longleftrightarrow I(m)\geq J(m)\;\text{for all}\;m\in\mathbb{N}.

Note that
\mathcal{I}
is indeed directed. For
I,J\in\mathcal{I}
define

K\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
by
K(m)=\min\{I(m),J(m)\}
.
Then
K\in\mathcal{I}
and
I,J\leq K
holds.

Lemma 13.9
.

(i)

Let
\underline{\phi}
be a morphism in
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*})
. Then there exists an admissible function

I\in\mathcal{I}
such that
\phi_{m}\in\mathcal{A}_{I(m)}
holds for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
;

(ii)

Let
\underline{\phi}
be a sequence of morphisms
\phi_{m}\colon A_{m}\to A_{m}^{\prime}
in
A_{0}
.
Suppose that there exists an admissible function

I\in\mathcal{I}
such that
\phi_{m}\in\mathcal{A}_{I(m)}
holds for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
. Then
\underline{\phi}
belongs to

\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*})
.

Proof.

(i)

Choose a function
L\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
such that
\phi_{m}
belongs to
\mathcal{A}_{l}
if

m\geq L(l)
. Define a new function
I^{\prime}\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
by

I^{\prime}(m)=\max\{i\in\{0,1,\ldots,m\}\mid\phi_{m}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}\}.

It satisfies

\begin{array}[]{lclcl}I^{\prime}(m)&\leq&m&&\text{for}\;m\in\mathbb{N};\\
l&\leq&I^{\prime}(m)&&\text{for}\;l,m\in\mathbb{N},m\geq L(l),m\geq l;\\
\phi_{m}&\in&\mathcal{A}_{I^{\prime}(m)}&&\text{for}\;m\in\mathbb{N}.\end{array}

Define the function
I\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
by

I(m)=\min\{I^{\prime}(j)\mid j\in\mathbb{N},m\leq j\}.

Then we get for all
n\in\mathbb{N}

\begin{array}[]{lclcl}I(m)&\leq&m&&\text{for}\;m\in\mathbb{N};\\
I(m)&\leq&I(m+1)&&\text{for}\;m\in\mathbb{N};\\
l&\leq&I(m)&&\text{for}\;l,m,\in\mathbb{N},m\geq L(l),m\geq l;\\
\phi_{m}&\in&\mathcal{A}_{I(m)}&&\text{for}\;m\in\mathbb{N}.\end{array}

The first three properties imply
I\in\mathcal{I}
.

(ii)

Suppose that there exists
I\in\mathcal{I}
satisfying
\phi_{m}\in\mathcal{A}_{I(m)}
for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
.
Define the desired function
L\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
by
L(l)=\min\{m\in\mathbb{N}\mid l\leq I(m)\}
.
∎

13.
b
.
Uniform regular coherence

Lemma 13.10
.

Consider the nested sequence
\mathcal{A}_{*}
of additive categories

\mathcal{A}_{0}\supseteq\mathcal{A}_{1}\supseteq\mathcal{A}_{2}\supseteq\cdots
.
Suppose that for the natural number
l\geq 2
each of the additive categories
\mathcal{A}_{m}
is
l
-uniformly
regular coherent and that the inclusion
\mathcal{A}_{m}\to\mathcal{A}_{m+1}
is flat for
m\in\mathbb{N}
.

Then
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*})
and
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{*})
are
l
-uniformly regular coherent.

Proof.

We first treat
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*})
.
Let
\underline{\phi}^{1}\colon\underline{A}^{1}\to\underline{A}^{0}
be a morphism in
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*})
.
Because of Lemma
13.9

(i)

we can choose
I\in\mathcal{I}
with
A^{1}_{m},A^{0}_{m},\phi^{1}_{m}\in\mathcal{A}_{I(m)}
.
By assumption we can find for each
m\in\mathbb{N}
an exact sequence

0\to A^{l}_{m}\xrightarrow{\phi^{l}_{m}}A^{l-1}_{m}\xrightarrow{\phi^{l}_{m}}\cdots\xrightarrow{\phi^{2}_{m}}A^{1}_{m}\xrightarrow{\phi^{1}_{1}}A^{0}_{m}

in
\mathcal{A}_{I(m)}
. We conclude from
Lemma
13.9

(ii)

that the collection of these sequences for
m=0,1,2\ldots

defines a sequence in
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*})

(13.11)

0\to\underline{A}^{l}\xrightarrow{\underline{\phi}^{l}}\underline{A}^{l-1}\xrightarrow{\underline{\phi}^{l-1}}\cdots\xrightarrow{\underline{\phi}^{2}}\underline{A}^{1}\xrightarrow{\underline{\phi}^{1}}\underline{A}^{0}.

Finally we show that the sequence (
13.11
) is exact as a
sequence in
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*})
. We have to solve for every

j\in\{1,\ldots,l\}
the following lifting problem in
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*})
.

(13.12)

\textstyle{\underline{A}^{j+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\underline{\phi}^{j+1}}
\textstyle{\underline{A}^{j}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\underline{\phi}^{j}}
\textstyle{\underline{A}^{j-1}}
\textstyle{\underline{B}.\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\underline{\nu}}
\scriptstyle{\underline{\mu}}
\scriptstyle{0}

Because of Lemma
13.9

(i)

we can choose
J\in\mathcal{I}
such
B_{m},\mu_{m}\in\mathcal{A}_{J(m)}
holds.
Choose
K\in\mathcal{I}
with
I,J\leq K
.
Now consider the following lifting problem in
\mathcal{A}_{K(m)}

(13.13)

\textstyle{A^{j+1}_{m}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi^{j+1}_{m}}
\textstyle{A^{j}_{m}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi^{j}_{m}}
\textstyle{A^{j-1}_{m}}
\textstyle{B_{m}.\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\nu_{m}}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{m}}
\scriptstyle{0}

As the inclusion

\mathcal{A}_{I(m)}\to\mathcal{A}_{K(m)}
is flat by assumption, and the sequence

A^{j+1}_{m}\xrightarrow{\phi^{j+1}_{m}}A^{j}_{m}\xrightarrow{\phi^{j}_{m}}A^{j-1}_{m}
is by construction exact at

A^{j}_{m}
, when considered in
\mathcal{A}_{I(m)}
, it is exact at

A^{j}_{m}
, when considered in
\mathcal{A}_{K(m)}
.
Hence (
13.13
) has a solution
\nu_{m}\colon B_{m}\to A^{j+1}

when considered in
\mathcal{A}_{K(m)}
. We conclude from
Lemma
13.9

(ii)

that the collection of the morphisms
\nu_{m}
yields a morphism

\underline{\nu}\colon\underline{B}\to\underline{A}^{j+1}

in
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*})
. Therefore
\overline{\nu}
is a solution to the lifting
problem (
13.12
) in
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*})
. We conclude that (
13.11
) is an
exact sequence in
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*})
. This finishes the proof of
Lemma
13.10
for
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*})
.

The proof for
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{*})
is the following modification of the one for
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*})
.
Let
\overline{\underline{\phi}^{1}}\colon\underline{A}^{1}\to\underline{A}^{0}
be a
morphism in
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{*})
. Choose a representative

\underline{\phi}^{1}\colon\underline{A}^{1}\to\underline{A}^{0}
in
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*})
.
Now one proceeds as above and constructs the sequence (
13.11
)
in
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*})
. However, instead of solving the lifting problem (
13.12
) in

\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*})
we have to solve the lifting problem

(13.14)

\textstyle{\underline{A}^{j+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{\underline{\phi}^{j+1}}}
\textstyle{\underline{A}^{j}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{\underline{\phi}^{j}}}
\textstyle{\underline{A}^{j-1}}
\textstyle{\underline{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\underline{\nu}}
\scriptstyle{\overline{\underline{\mu}}}
\scriptstyle{0}

in
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{*})
. Choose a representative
\underline{\mu}
for
\overline{\underline{\mu}}
.
There is a natural number
M
such that
\phi^{j}_{m}\circ\mu_{m}=0
holds for

m\geq M
. We can change the representative
\underline{\mu}

by putting
\mu_{m}=0
for
m<M
and by leaving
\mu_{m}
unchanged for
m\geq M
.
Now we choose a solution
\nu_{m}
to the lifting problem (
13.13
) in
\mathcal{A}_{K(m)}
for
m\geq M
.
Put
\nu_{m}=0
for
m<M
. Then we get a morphism

\underline{\nu}\colon\underline{B}\to\underline{A}^{j+1}

in
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*})
such that its class
\overline{\underline{\nu}}\colon\underline{B}\to\underline{A}^{j+1}
in
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{*})

is a solution to the lifting problem (
13.14
). This finishes the proof of
Lemma
13.10
.
∎

Example 13.15

(The property Noetherian does not pass to the sequence category)
.

The analogue of Lemma
13.10
for the properties
Noetherian, regular, or
l
-uniformly regular instead of uniformly
l
-regular coherent does not hold as the
following example shows. Suppose that none of the
\mathcal{A}_{m}
is the trivial additive
category. Consider an object
\underline{A}
of
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*})
such that

A_{m}\not=\{0\}
for
m\in\mathbb{N}
, and the
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*})
-module

F=\mor_{\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*})}(\underline{?},\underline{A}).

Define a
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*})
-submodule
V
of
F
by

V(?)=\{\underline{\phi}\in F(?)\mid\exists M(\underline{\phi})\in\mathbb{N}\>\text{with}\;\phi_{m}=0\;\text{for}\;m\geq M(\underline{\phi})\}.

Suppose that there exists an object
\underline{B}
and an epimorphism

f\colon\mor_{\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*})}(\underline{?},\underline{B})\to V
. If we write

f(\id_{\underline{B}})=\underline{\psi}\in V(\underline{B})
, then there must be a natural
number
M
with
\psi_{m}=0
for
m\geq M
. This implies that for any

\underline{\phi}\in V(?)
we have
\phi_{m}=0
for
m\geq M
. This is a contradiction,
since
M
does not depend on
\underline{\phi}
. Hence
V
is not finitely generated and

\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*})
is not Noetherian. This construction yields also a counterexample for

\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{*})
.

13.
c
.
The algebraic
K
-theory of the sequence categories

\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*})
,
\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{A}_{*})
and
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{*})

Given
I\in\mathcal{I}
, define a subcategory

\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*})_{I}
of
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*})
as follows. An object
\underline{A}
in

\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*})
belongs to
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*})_{I}
, if
A_{m}\in\mathcal{A}_{I(m)}
holds for

m\in\mathbb{N}
. A morphism
\underline{\phi}\colon\underline{A}\to\underline{T}
in

\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*})
belongs to
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*})_{I}
, if and only if
\phi_{m}\in\mathcal{A}_{I(m)}

holds for
m\in\mathbb{N}
.

Next we show

(13.16)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*})_{I}

\displaystyle\subseteq

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*})_{J}\quad\text{for}\;I,J\in\mathcal{I},I\leq J;

(13.17)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigcup_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*})_{I}.

The first equation is obvious since
\mathcal{A}_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{j}
for
i\geq j
.
The second follows from Lemma
13.9
.

Define analogously the subcategory
\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{A}_{*})_{I}
of
\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{A}_{*})
. Then we get

(13.18)

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{A}_{*})_{I}

\displaystyle\subseteq

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{A}_{*})_{J}\quad\text{for}\;I,J\in\mathcal{I},I\leq J;

(13.19)

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{A}_{*})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigcup_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{A}_{*})_{I}.

One easily checks that the inclusion
\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{A}_{*})_{I}\subseteq\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*})_{I}
is Karoubi filtration. Define

(13.20)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{*})_{I}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*})_{I}/\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{A}_{*})_{I}.

Then we get

(13.21)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{*})_{I}

\displaystyle\subseteq

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{*})_{J}\quad\text{for}\;I,J\in\mathcal{I},I\leq J;

(13.22)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{*})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigcup_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{*})_{I}.

Lemma 13.23
.

(i)

We get for
I,J\in\mathcal{I}
with
I\leq J
functors

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(A_{*})_{I}

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(A_{*})_{J};

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(A_{*})_{I}

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(A_{*}),

and analogously for
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{L}
;

(ii)

The functors appearing
in assertion
(i)

induce weak homotopy equivalences, natural in
\mathcal{A}_{*}

\displaystyle\hocolim_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbf{K}^{\infty}(\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*})_{I})

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\simeq}

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}^{\infty}(\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*}));

\displaystyle\hocolim_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbf{K}^{\infty}(\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{A}_{*})_{I})

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\simeq}

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}^{\infty}(\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{A}_{*}));

\displaystyle\hocolim_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbf{K}^{\infty}(\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{*})_{I})

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\simeq}

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}^{\infty}(\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{*})).

Proof.

(i)

The desired functors come from (
13.16
), (
13.18
),
and (
13.21
).

(ii)

This follows from
of (
13.17
) (
13.19
), (
13.22
)
and
[
13
, Corollary 7.2]
.
∎

Given
I\in\mathcal{I}
, we define
\bigoplus_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{A}_{I(m)}
to be the full
subcategory of
\prod_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{A}_{I(m)}
consisting of those objects

\underline{A}
for which there exists a natural number
M
(depending on
\underline{A}
)
satisfying
A_{m}=0
for
m\geq M
. Let

\bigl(\prod_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{A}_{I(m)}\bigr)\bigl/\bigl(\bigoplus_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{A}_{I(m)}\bigr)

be the quotient additive category.

Lemma 13.24
.

Fix
I\in\mathcal{I}
. There are weak homotopy equivalences, natural in
\mathcal{A}_{*}
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}^{\infty}\bigl(\prod_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{A}_{I(m)}\bigr)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\simeq}

\displaystyle\prod_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbf{K}^{\infty}\bigl(\mathcal{A}_{I(m)}\bigr);

\displaystyle\bigvee_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbf{K}^{\infty}(\mathcal{A}_{I(m)}\bigr)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\simeq}

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}^{\infty}\bigl(\bigoplus_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{A}_{I(m)}\bigr),

and

\hocofib\bigl(\mathbf{K}^{\infty}\bigl(\bigoplus_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{A}_{I(m)}\bigr)\to\mathbf{K}^{\infty}\bigl(\prod_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{A}_{I(m)}\bigr)\bigr)\\
\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathbf{K}^{\infty}\left(\prod_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{A}_{I(m)}\biggl/\bigoplus_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{A}_{I(m)}\right).

Proof.

The first one is weak homotopy equivalence by
[
4
]
, see
also
[
7
, Theorem 1.2]
, since the non-connective algebraic

K
-theory spectrum is indeed an
\Omega
-spectrum. The second one is a weak homotopy
equivalence, since
\bigoplus_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{A}_{m}
is the union of the subcategories

\bigoplus_{m=0}^{n}\mathcal{A}_{i}
and hence we get a weak homotopy equivalence

\hocolim_{n\to\infty}\mathbf{K}^{\infty}\bigl(\bigoplus_{m=0}^{n}\mathcal{A}_{I(m)}\bigr)\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathbf{K}^{\infty}\bigl(\bigoplus_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{A}_{I(m)}\bigr),

and the natural map

\bigvee_{m=0}^{n}\mathbf{K}(\mathcal{A}_{I(m)})\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathbf{K}^{\infty}\bigl(\bigoplus_{m=0}^{n}\mathcal{A}_{I(m)}\bigr)

is a weak homotopy equivalence. The third map is a weak homotopy equivalence, since
the inclusion
\bigoplus_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{A}_{I(m)}\subseteq\prod_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{A}_{I(m)}
is a Karoubi filtration.
∎

Lemma 13.25
.

Given
I\in\mathcal{I}
, there are weak homotopy equivalences, natural in
\mathcal{A}_{*}
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}^{\infty}\bigl(\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*})_{I}\bigr)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\simeq}

\displaystyle\prod_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbf{K}^{\infty}\bigl(\mathcal{A}_{I(m)}\bigr);

\displaystyle\bigvee_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbf{K}^{\infty}(\mathcal{A}_{I(m)}\bigr)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\simeq}
