\displaystyle\mathbf{K}^{\infty}\bigl(\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{A}_{*})_{I}\bigr);

\displaystyle\hocofib\bigl(\mathbf{K}^{\infty}\bigl(\bigoplus_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{A}_{I(m)}\bigr)\to\mathbf{K}^{\infty}\bigl(\prod_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{A}_{I(m)}\bigr)\bigr)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\simeq}

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}^{\infty}(\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})_{I}).

Proof.

The are obvious identifications

\displaystyle\prod_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{A}_{I(m)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*})_{I};

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{A}_{I(m)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{A}_{*})_{I};

\displaystyle\prod_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{A}_{I(m)}\biggl/\bigoplus_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{A}_{I(m)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{*})_{I}.

Now the claim follows from Lema
13.24
.
∎

As a consequence of
Lemma
13.23

(ii)

and
Lemma
13.25

we get

Lemma 13.26

(
K
-groups of
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*})
,
\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{A}_{*})
, and
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{*})
)
.

There are zigzags of weak homotopy equivalences of spectra, natural in
\mathcal{A}_{*}

\displaystyle\hocolim_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\prod_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbf{K}^{\infty}\bigl(\mathcal{A}_{I(m)}\bigr)

\displaystyle\xleftrightarrow{\simeq}

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}^{\infty}\bigl(\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*})\bigr);

\displaystyle\hocolim_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\bigvee_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbf{K}^{\infty}(\mathcal{A}_{I(m)}\bigr)

\displaystyle\xleftrightarrow{\simeq}

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}^{\infty}\bigl(\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{A}_{*})\bigr);

\displaystyle\hocolim_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\left(\hocofib\bigl(\mathbf{K}^{\infty}\bigl(\bigoplus_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{A}_{I(m)}\bigr)\to\mathbf{K}^{\infty}\bigl(\prod_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{A}_{I(m)}\bigr)\bigr)\right)

\displaystyle\xleftrightarrow{\simeq}

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}^{\infty}(\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})).

In particular we get from Lemma
13.26

for every
n\in\mathbb{Z}
an isomorphism

(13.27)

\displaystyle\colim_{i\in I}\biggl(\prod_{m\in\mathbb{N}}K_{n}(A_{I(m)})\biggl/\bigoplus_{m\in\mathbb{N}}K_{n}(A_{I(m)})\biggr)

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle K_{n}(\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{*})).

14.
The main technical result

14.
a
.
The statement of the main technical result

Fix a natural number
r
and a nested sequence of additive categories
\mathcal{A}_{*}
. We have
defined the additive category
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{*})
in
Definition
13.3
. Suppose that each
\mathcal{A}_{*}
comes with a

\mathbb{Z}^{r}
-action
\Psi\colon\mathbb{Z}^{r}\to\proaut(\mathcal{A}_{*})
by pro-automorphisms in the sense
of Definition
13.2
. Then
we obtain a
\mathbb{Z}^{r}
-action
\Phi\colon\mathbb{Z}^{r}\to\aut(\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{*}))
on

\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{*})
see (
13.7
). We obtain a covariant functor, see for
instance
[
1
, Section 9]
,

\mathbf{K}^{\infty}_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{*})}\colon\matheurm{Or}(\mathbb{Z}^{r})\to\matheurm{Spectra}.

It determines a
\mathbb{Z}^{r}
-homology theory
H_{n}^{\mathbb{Z}^{r}}(-,\mathbf{K}^{\infty}_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{*})})

with the property that for every subgroup
H\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{r}
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
we have the
natural isomorphisms

H_{n}^{\mathbb{Z}^{r}}(\mathbb{Z}^{r}/H,\mathbf{K}^{\infty}_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{*})})\xrightarrow{\cong}K_{n}(\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{*})\rtimes_{\Phi|_{H}}H)

as explained for instance in
[
1
, Section 9]
. The
nested sequence of additive categories
\mathcal{A}_{*}
yields for any natural number
d
another
nested sequence of additive categories
\mathcal{A}_{*}[\mathbb{Z}^{d}]
by

\mathcal{A}_{0}[\mathbb{Z}^{d}]\supseteq\mathcal{A}_{1}[\mathbb{Z}^{d}]\supseteq\mathcal{A}_{2}[\mathbb{Z}^{d}]\supseteq\cdots
, where

\mathcal{A}_{m}[\mathbb{Z}]
is the untwisted case of Definition
2.3
and we define
inductively
\mathcal{A}_{m}[\mathbb{Z}^{d}]=(\mathcal{A}_{m}[\mathbb{Z}^{d-1}])[\mathbb{Z}]
. Moreover,

\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{*}[\mathbb{Z}^{d}])
inherits a
\mathbb{Z}^{r}
-action

\Phi[\mathbb{Z}^{d}]\colon\mathbb{Z}^{r}\to\aut(\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{*}[\mathbb{Z}^{d}]))
.

The main result of this section is

Theorem 14.1
.

Suppose:

(i)

For every natural number
d
there exists a natural number
l(d)
such that for any natural
number
m
the additive category
\mathcal{A}_{m}[\mathbb{Z}^{d}]
is

l(d)
-uniformly regular coherent;

(ii)

The inclusion
\mathcal{A}_{m+1}[\mathbb{Z}^{d}]\to\mathcal{A}_{m}[\mathbb{Z}^{d}]
is exact for any natural numbers
d
and
m
.

Then the map induced by the projection
E\mathbb{Z}^{r}\to\pt

H_{n}^{\mathbb{Z}^{r}}(E\mathbb{Z}^{r};\mathbf{K}^{\infty}_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{*})})\to H_{n}^{\mathbb{Z}^{r}}(\pt;\mathbf{K}^{\infty}_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{*})})=K_{n}(\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{*})\rtimes_{\phi}\mathbb{Z}^{r})

is bijective for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.

14.
b
.
Reduction to the case
r=1

Lemma 14.2
.

If Theorem
14.1
holds for
r=1
,
it is true for all
r\geq 1
.

Proof.

The Farrell-Jones Conjecture is known to be true for
\mathbb{Z}^{r}

and implies that the map induced by the projection
E_{{{\mathcal{V}}\mathrm{cyc}}}(\mathbb{Z}^{r})\to\pt

H_{n}^{\mathbb{Z}^{r}}(E_{{{\mathcal{V}}\mathrm{cyc}}}(\mathbb{Z}^{r});\mathbf{K}^{\infty}_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{*})})\to H_{n}^{\mathbb{Z}^{r}}(\pt;\mathbf{K}^{\infty}_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{*})})

is an isomorphism for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, where
E_{{{\mathcal{V}}\mathrm{cyc}}}(\mathbb{Z}^{r})
is the classifying space
of the family
{{\mathcal{V}}\mathrm{cyc}}
of virtually cyclic subgroups of
G
, see for
instance
[
10
]
. We also have the map induced by the up to
\mathbb{Z}^{r}
-homotopy
unique
\mathbb{Z}^{r}
-map
E\mathbb{Z}^{r}\to E_{{{\mathcal{V}}\mathrm{cyc}}}(\mathbb{Z}^{r})
.

(14.3)

H_{n}^{\mathbb{Z}^{r}}(E\mathbb{Z}^{r};\mathbf{K}^{\infty}_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{*})})\to H_{n}^{\mathbb{Z}^{r}}(E_{{{\mathcal{V}}\mathrm{cyc}}}(\mathbb{Z}^{r});\mathbf{K}^{\infty}_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{*})}).

Obviously it suffices to show that (
14.3
) is bijective
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. By the Transitivity Principle, see for instance
[
12
, Theorem 65 on page 742]
,
this boils down to show that for any non-trivial virtually cyclic subgroup
V
of
\mathbb{Z}^{m}
the map
induced by the projection
EV\to\pt

H_{n}^{V}(EV;\mathbf{K}^{\infty}_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{*})}|_{V})\to H_{n}^{V}(\pt;\mathbf{K}^{\infty}_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{*})}|_{V})

is bijective for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. Since any non-trivial virtually cyclic subgroup
V
of

\mathbb{Z}^{r}
is isomorphic
\mathbb{Z}
, we have reduced the proof of
Theorem
14.1
to the special case
r=1
.
∎

14.
c
.
Strategy of proof of Theorem
14.1

We first explain, why the proof of
Theorem
14.1
is more
difficult than we had expected and the reader may anticipate. There are are the following
reasons.

•

There is no proof of the Devissage Theorem for non-connective
K
-theory.

We have used the Devissage Theorem for connective
K
-theory in the proof of
Lemma
7.3
. If
Devissage would hold also in the non-connective setting, the proof of
Lemma
7.3
could be extended to
the non-connective
K
-theory spectrum and hence
Lemma
7.7

and Theorem
7.8
would still
be true, if we replace the assumption that
\mathcal{A}[\mathbb{Z}^{m}]
is regular coherent for every

m\geq 0
by the weaker assumption that that
\mathcal{A}
is regular coherent.

Then the stronger version of
Theorem
14.1
, where one demands
the conditions only for
d=0
, would follow from the version of
Theorem
7.8
mentioned above,
and Lemma
13.10
, and
Lemma
14.2
.

Since the known proofs of the Devissage Theorem for non-connective
K
-theory make
strong assumptions on the underlying categories, which are not at all true in our case,
we cannot argue like this. The consequence is that we need to make regularity
assumptions about
\mathcal{A}_{m}[\mathbb{Z}^{d}]
for all
d\geq 0
and not only for
d=0
.

•

If the additive category
\mathcal{A}
is regular coherent, it is unknown whether
\mathcal{A}[\mathbb{Z}]

is regular coherent.

It is an open well-known problem, whether for a regular coherent ring
R
the ring

R[\mathbb{Z}^{d}]
is again regular coherent. Hence we do not know, whether for a regular
coherent additive category
\mathcal{A}
, the additive category
\mathcal{A}[\mathbb{Z}^{d}]
is regular
coherent again.

•

The additive category
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{*})
is not Noetherian and hence not regular.

We have shown that for a regular additive category
\mathcal{A}
, the additive category

\mathcal{A}[\mathbb{Z}^{d}]
is regular again, see Theorem
10.1
.
However, the additive category
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{*})
is not Noetherian and hence not regular,
see Example
13.15
.

•

The canonical inclusion
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{*})[\mathbb{Z}^{d}]\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{*}[\mathbb{Z}^{d}])
is not
an equivalence of additive categories for
d\geq 1
3
3

3

The inclusion is bijective on objects, but not on morphisms.
.

The assumptions on the
\mathcal{A}_{m}
imply that
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{*}[\mathbb{Z}^{d}])
is regular coherent, but give us no information
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{*})[\mathbb{Z}^{d}]
.
For this reason we cannot use the fact (coming from the Bass-Heller-Swan decomposition) that
K_{n}(\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{*}))
is a direct summand of
K_{n+d}(\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{*})[\mathbb{Z}^{d}])
to deduce Theorem
14.1
for all
n
from the case
n>>0
.
On the other hand for
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{*}[\mathbb{Z}^{d}])
we do not have a Bass-Heller-Swan decomposition
4
4

4

It seems not obvious how a Bass-Heller-Swan decomposition for
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{*}[\mathbb{Z}^{d}])
should look like and what the Nil terms should be.
.
But we will exhibit
K_{n}(\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{*}))
also as a direct summand of
K_{n+d}(\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{*}[\mathbb{Z}^{d}]))
and this will be the main work in the remainder of this paper.
To this end we will check that enough of the arguments used in
[
14
]
to construct the Bass-Heller-Swan decomposition can also be applied to
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{*}[\mathbb{Z}^{d}])
and yield the desired direct summand.

14.
d
.
Twisted Laurent categories

Recall the notion of a (twisted) finite Laurent category
from Definition
2.3
. Given a sequences of additive subcategories
\mathcal{A}_{*}
,
define the new sequences of additive subcategories
\mathcal{A}_{*}[t^{\pm}]
and
\mathcal{A}_{*}[t,t^{\pm}]

by replacing
\mathcal{A}_{m}
by the associated untwisted finite
Laurent categories
\mathcal{A}_{m}[t^{\pm}]
and
\mathcal{A}_{m}[t,t^{-1}]
. A pro-automorphism

\Phi\colon\mathcal{A}_{*}\to\mathcal{A}_{*}
defines pro-automorphisms

\Phi[t^{\pm}]\colon\mathcal{A}_{*}[t^{\pm}]\to\mathcal{A}_{*}[t^{\pm}]
and

\Phi[t,t^{{-1}}]\colon\mathcal{A}_{*}[t,t^{{-1}}]\to\mathcal{A}_{*}[t,t^{{-1}}]
. Define

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}(\Phi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*})_{\mathcal{S}(\Phi)}[\mathbb{Z}];

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}[t^{\pm}](\Phi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*}[t^{\pm}])_{\mathcal{S}(\Phi[t^{\pm}])}[\mathbb{Z}];

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}[t,t^{-1}](\Phi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*}[t,t^{-1}])_{\mathcal{S}(\Phi[t,t^{-1}])}[\mathbb{Z}].

The passage from
\Phi
to
\mathcal{C}(\Phi)
,
\mathcal{C}[t^{\pm}](\Phi)
, and

\mathcal{C}[t,t^{-1}](\Phi)
is natural. Often we omit
\Phi
from the
notation. The set of objects of the categories
\mathcal{C}(\Phi)
,
\mathcal{C}[t^{\pm}](\Phi)
,
and
\mathcal{C}[t,t^{-1}](\Phi)
can and will be identified with the set of
objects of
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*})
and hence are independent of
\Phi
.

14.
e
.
Induction functors

Next we define a commutative square of additive categories

(14.4)

\textstyle{\mathcal{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{+}}
\scriptstyle{i_{-}}
\scriptstyle{i_{0}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{C}[t]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j_{+}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{C}[t^{-1}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j_{-}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{C}[t,t^{-1}]}

where the functors
i_{0}
,
i_{+}
,
i_{-}
,
j_{-}
, and
j_{+}
induce the identity on the set of
objects. For each additive category
\mathcal{A}_{m}
.we have a diagram of functors of untwisted Laurent
categories, where all functors are the canoncial ones, see
[
14
, Section 1.4]
,

\textstyle{\mathcal{A}_{m}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{+}(\mathcal{A}_{m})}
\scriptstyle{i_{-}(\mathcal{A}_{m})}
\scriptstyle{i_{0}(\mathcal{A}_{m})}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}_{m}[t]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j_{+}(\mathcal{A}_{m})}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}_{m}[t^{-1}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j_{-}(\mathcal{A}_{m})}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}_{m}[t,t^{-1}].}

This construction is natural with respect to the inclusions
\mathcal{A}_{m+1}\to\mathcal{A}_{m}
.
Hence we obtain a commutative diagram of additive categories

\textstyle{\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{S}(i_{+}(\mathcal{A}_{*}))}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{S}(i_{-}(\mathcal{A}_{*}))}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{S}(i_{0}(\mathcal{A}_{*}))}
\textstyle{\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*}[t])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{S}(j_{+}(\mathcal{A}_{*}))}
\textstyle{\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*})[t^{-1}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{S}(j_{-}(\mathcal{A}_{*}))}
\textstyle{\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*}[t,t^{-1}]).}

Since it is compatible with the automorphisms of additive categories

\mathcal{S}(\Phi)
of
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*})
,
\mathcal{S}(\Phi[t^{\pm}])
of
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*}[t^{\pm}])
,
and
\mathcal{S}(\Phi[t,t^{-1}])
of
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*}[t,t^{-1}])
,
it yield the desired diagram (
14.4
) in the obvious way.

Note that the diagram diagram (
14.4
) is natural in
\Phi\colon\mathcal{A}_{*}\to\mathcal{A}_{*}
.

14.
f
.
Formally adjoining infinite direct sums

In
[
14
, Section 1.3]
a functorial extension

\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{A}^{\kappa}
is constructed for every additive category
\mathcal{A}

such that
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{A}^{\kappa}
is an inclusion of additive categories and
in
\mathcal{A}^{\kappa}
the direct sums over a collection of objects over a countable set
is defined. Now define
for a nested sequence of additive categories a functor of additive categories

\mathcal{A}_{*}
given by
\mathcal{A}_{0}\supseteq\mathcal{A}_{1}\supseteq\mathcal{A}_{2}\supseteq\cdots

the nested sequence of additive categories
\mathcal{A}_{*}^{\kappa}
by

\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\kappa}\supseteq\mathcal{A}_{1}^{\kappa}\supseteq\mathcal{A}_{2}^{\kappa}\supseteq\cdots
.
The given pro-automorphism
\phi\colon\mathcal{A}_{*}\to\mathcal{A}_{*}
yields
a pro-automorphism
\phi^{\kappa}\colon\mathcal{A}_{*}^{\kappa}\to\mathcal{A}_{*}^{\kappa}

by passing to
\phi^{\kappa}\colon\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\kappa}\to\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\kappa}
. Define

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{\kappa}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*}^{\kappa})_{\mathcal{S}(\Phi^{\kappa})}[\mathbb{Z}];

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}[t^{\pm}]^{\kappa}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*}[t^{\pm}]^{\kappa})_{\mathcal{S}(\Phi[t^{\pm}]^{\kappa})}[\mathbb{Z}];

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}[t,t^{-1}]^{\kappa}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*}[t,t^{-1}]^{\kappa})_{\mathcal{S}(\Phi^{\kappa}[t,t^{-1}]^{\kappa})}[\mathbb{Z}].

Note that we are working with
\mathcal{A}_{m}[t^{\pm}]^{\kappa}
, which is different from

(\mathcal{A}_{m}^{\kappa})[t^{\pm}]
. The same comment applies to

\mathcal{A}_{m}[t,t^{-1}]^{\kappa}
, which is different from
(\mathcal{A}_{m}^{\kappa})[t,t^{-1}]
.

The diagram (
14.4
) extends in the obvious way to
the diagram, natural in
\Phi
.

(14.5)

\textstyle{\mathcal{C}^{\kappa}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{+}^{\kappa}}
\scriptstyle{i_{-}^{\kappa}}
\scriptstyle{i_{0}^{\kappa}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{C}[t]^{\kappa}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j_{+}^{\kappa}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{C}[t^{-1}]^{\kappa}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j_{-}^{\kappa}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{C}[t,t^{-1}]^{\kappa}.}

14.
g
.
Restriction functors

In
[
14
, Section 1.5]
restriction functors are defined for
each additive category
\mathcal{A}_{m}

\displaystyle i^{0}(\mathcal{A}_{m})\colon\mathcal{A}_{m}[t,t^{-1}]^{\kappa}

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{m}^{\kappa};

\displaystyle i^{\pm}(\mathcal{A}_{m})\colon\mathcal{A}_{m}[t^{\pm 1}]^{\kappa}

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{m}^{\kappa},

such that they come with adjunctions
(i_{0}(\mathcal{A}_{m})^{\kappa},i^{0}(\mathcal{A}_{m}))
,

(i_{+}(\mathcal{A}_{m})^{\kappa},i^{+}(\mathcal{A}_{m}))
, and
(i_{-}(\mathcal{A}_{m})^{\kappa},i^{-}(\mathcal{A}_{m}))
.
Moreover, everything is natural and in particular compatible with the automorphisms

\Phi[t,t^{-1}]^{\kappa}\colon\mathcal{A}_{0}[t,t^{-1}]^{\kappa}\to\mathcal{A}_{0}[t,t^{-1}]^{\kappa}
and

\Phi[t^{\pm}]^{\kappa}\colon\mathcal{A}_{0}[t^{\pm}]^{\kappa}\to\mathcal{A}_{0}[t^{\pm}]^{\kappa}
. Hence these data define analogously to the construction of
Subsection
14.e
restriction functors

(14.6)

\displaystyle i^{0}\colon\mathcal{C}[t,t^{-1}]^{\kappa}

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{\kappa};

(14.7)

\displaystyle i^{\pm}\colon\mathcal{C}[t^{\pm 1}]^{\kappa}

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{\kappa},

such that we have adjunctions
((i_{0})^{\kappa},i^{0})
,
((i_{+})^{\kappa},i^{+})
, and
((i_{-})^{\kappa},i^{-})
.
Everything is natural in
\Phi
.

14.
h
.
Truncation functors

Put

\overline{\mathbb{Z}}:=\mathbb{Z}\amalg\{-\infty,\infty\}.

Notation 14.8

(Truncation for objects)
.

Let
\underline{A}=(A_{m})_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
and
\underline{A^{\prime}}=(A_{m}^{\prime})_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
be
objects in
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*})
. Consider elements
\underline{a}=(a_{m})_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
and

\underline{b}=(b_{m})_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
in
\prod_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
. Define an
object in
\mathcal{C}^{\kappa}
by

\underline{A}[\underline{a},\underline{b}]=\left\{\left.\bigoplus_{k_{i}=a_{i}}^{b_{i}}A_{m}\;\right|\;m\in\mathbb{N}\right\},

where
\bigoplus_{k_{i}=a_{i}}^{b_{i}}A_{m}
is to be defined to be zero if
a_{i}>b_{i}
or if
we have
a_{i}=b_{i}
and
b_{i}\in\{\pm\infty\}
. Note that the direct sum

\bigoplus_{k_{i}=a_{i}}^{b_{i}}A_{m}
has as entry for
k_{i}
always the same object, namely

A_{m}
. Since we are working in
\mathcal{C}^{\kappa}
, this definition makes sense also in
the case where
a_{i}=-\infty
or
b_{i}=\infty
.

Given an element
c\in\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
, denote by
\underline{c}
the
element in
\prod_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
, whose value at every
m\in\mathbb{N}

is
c
. Then we get for any object
\underline{A}
in
\mathcal{C}[t,t^{-1}]
, which is the same
as an object in
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*})
,

i^{0}\underline{A}=\underline{A}[\underline{-\infty},\underline{\infty}].

Given a morphism
\underline{f}\colon\underline{A}\to\underline{A^{\prime}}
in

\mathcal{C}[t,t^{-1}]
and
\underline{a},\underline{b}
,
\underline{a^{\prime}}
, and

\underline{b^{\prime}}
in
\prod_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
, define the
\mathcal{C}^{\kappa}
-morphism

f[\,]\colon\underline{A}[\underline{a},\underline{b}]\to\underline{A^{\prime}}[\underline{a^{\prime}},\underline{b^{\prime}}]

to be the composite

\underline{f}[\,]\colon\underline{A}[\underline{a},\underline{b}]\xrightarrow{\underline{i}}\underline{A}[\underline{-\infty},\underline{\infty}]=i^{0}\underline{A}\xrightarrow{i^{0}\underline{f}}\underline{A^{\prime}}[\underline{-\infty},\underline{\infty}]=i^{0}\underline{A^{\prime}}\xrightarrow{\underline{p}}\underline{A^{\prime}}[\underline{a^{\prime}},\underline{b^{\prime}}],

where
\underline{i}
is the obvious inclusion and
\underline{p}
is the obvious projection in
\mathcal{C}^{\kappa}
.

The morphism

f[\,]\colon i^{0}\underline{A}=A[\underline{-\infty},\underline{\infty}]\to i^{0}\underline{A^{\prime}}=A^{\prime}[\underline{-\infty},\underline{\infty}]
agrees with
i^{0}f
for a
morphism
\underline{f}\colon\underline{A}\to\underline{A^{\prime}}
in
\mathcal{C}[t,t^{-1}]
. If

f
belongs to
\mathcal{C}[t^{\pm}]
, we abbreviate
(i_{\pm}f)[\,]
by
f[\,]
again.

Note that
(\underline{g}\circ\underline{f})[\,]
is in general
not
equal to

\underline{g}[\,]\circ\underline{f}[\,]
and
\id[\,]
is in general
not
the
identity. As a typical example, let
\underline{f^{+}}\colon\underline{A}\to\underline{A}

be the morphism
\id_{\underline{A}}\cdot t
and

\underline{f^{-}}\colon\underline{A}\to\underline{A}
be the morphism

\id_{\underline{A}}\cdot t^{-1}
.Then

(f^{+}\circ f^{-})[\,]\colon A[0,\underline{\infty}]\to A[0,\underline{\infty}]

is the identity. The map

f^{-}[\,]\colon\underline{A}[\underline{0},\underline{\infty}]\to\underline{A}[\underline{0},\underline{\infty}]

is given at each
m\in\mathbb{N}
by the map

\bigoplus_{k=0}^{\infty}A_{m}\to\bigoplus_{k=0}^{\infty}A_{m}
sending

\{u_{k}\mid k=0,1,2,\ldots\}
to
\{u_{k+1}\mid k=0,1,2,\ldots\}
.
Since
f^{-}[\,]
is not injective, we do
not
have

f^{+}[\,]\circ f^{-}[\,]=(f^{+}\circ f^{-})[\,]
.

As another example,

\id_{\underline{A}}[\,]\colon\underline{A}[\underline{-\infty},\underline{\infty}]\to A[\underline{0},\underline{0}]

is given at every
m\in\mathbb{N}
by the projection onto the
0
th summand

\bigoplus_{k=-\infty}^{\infty}A_{m}\to A_{m}
.

Notation 14.9

(Truncation for chain complexes)
.

If
C^{+}
is a
\mathcal{C}[t]
-chain complex and

\underline{a},\underline{b}\in\prod_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
, then
we obtain a
\mathcal{C}^{\kappa}
-chain complex
C^{+}[\underline{a},\underline{b}]
by defining
the
n
th chain object to be
C^{+}_{n}[\underline{a},\underline{b}]
and the
n
-th
differential to be

c_{n}[\,]\colon C^{+}_{n}[\underline{a},\underline{b}]\to C^{+}_{n-1}[\underline{a},\underline{b}]
, if
c_{n}
is the differential of
C^{+}
. (One
has to check that
c_{n}[\,]\circ c_{n+1}[\,]=0
.) A chain map

\underline{f}\colon C^{+}\to D^{+}
of
\mathcal{C}[t]
-chain complexes induces a

\mathcal{C}^{\kappa}
-chain map denoted by

f[\,]\colon C^{+}[\underline{a},\underline{b}]\to D^{+}[\underline{a^{\prime}},\underline{b^{\prime}}]
,
provided that
\underline{a^{\prime}}\leq\underline{a}
, i.e.,
a^{\prime}_{m}\leq a_{m}
for all

m\in\mathbb{N}
, and
\underline{b^{\prime}}\leq\underline{b}
hold.

If
C^{-}
is an
\mathcal{C}[t^{-1}]
-chain complex and

\underline{a},\underline{b}\in\prod_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
,
define the
\mathcal{C}^{\kappa}
-chain complex
C^{-}[\underline{a},\underline{b}]
analogously.
A chain map
f\colon C^{-}\to D^{-}
of
\mathcal{A}[t^{-1}]
-chain complexes induces a

\mathcal{C}^{\kappa}
-chain map denoted by

f[\,]\colon C^{-}[\underline{a},\underline{b}]\to D^{-}[\underline{a^{\prime}},\underline{b^{\prime}}]
,
provided that
\underline{a^{\prime}}\geq\underline{a}
, i.e.,
a^{\prime}_{m}\geq a_{m}
for all

m\in\mathbb{N}
, and
\underline{b^{\prime}}\geq\underline{b}
hold.

Note that Notation
14.9

(in contrast to Notation
14.8
)
does in this generality
not
make sense
for chain complexes in
\mathcal{C}[t,t^{-1}]
, e.g.,

c_{n}[\,]\circ c_{n+1}[\,]=0
does not hold anymore.

14.
i
.
Some basic tools for non-connective
K
-theory

Recall that we have defined the negative
K
-theory of an additive category using the
delooping construction based on the Bass-Heller-Swan decomposition
of
[
13
]
. In this section we present another definition
based on the non-connective
K
-theory spectrum associated to appropriate Waldhausen
categories due to Bunke-Kasprowski-Winges
[
2
]
.

The next definition is taken from
[
2
, Definition 2.1]
.

Definition 14.10
.

(i)

The
Waldhausen category
\mathcal{W}
admits
factorizations
, if every morphism in
\mathcal{W}

can be factorized into a cofibration followed by a weak equivalence; no functoriality
of this factorization is assumed;

(ii)

The Waldhausen category
\mathcal{W}
is
homotopical
, if it admits factorizations
and the weak equivalences satisfy the
two-out-of-six property
, i.e., if for
composable morphisms

C_{0}\xrightarrow{f_{1}}C_{1}\xrightarrow{f_{2}}C_{2}\xrightarrow{f_{3}}C_{3}
in
\mathcal{W}

both
f_{2}\circ f_{1}
and
f_{3}\circ f_{2}
are weak equivalences, then also

f_{1}
,
f_{2}
,
f_{3}
, and
f_{3}\circ f_{2}\circ f_{1}
are weak equivalences.

Let
\matheurm{Wald}^{\operatorname{ho}}
be the category of homotopical Waldhausen categories.
In the sequel we denote by

(14.11)

{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}\colon\matheurm{Wald}^{\operatorname{ho}}\to\matheurm{Spectra}

the non-connective
K
-theory functor constructed in
[
2
, Definition 2.37]
.

Remark 14.12
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an additive category. Then
\mathcal{A}
becomes a Waldhausen category, if we
define the weak equivalences to be the isomorphisms and the cofibration to be the
morphisms
f\colon A\to B
, for which there exists an object
A^{\perp}
and an
isomorphism
u\colon A\oplus A^{\perp}\xrightarrow{\cong}{B}
such that the
composite of
u
with the canonical inclusion
A\to A\oplus A^{\perp}
is
f
. Note that
this Waldhausen category is
not
homotopical, as it does not satisfy
factorization. So we cannot apply (
14.11
) to the
Waldhausen category
\mathcal{A}
.

Let
\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
be the Waldhausen category of bounded chain complexes over
\mathcal{A}
,
where a cofibration
f_{*}\colon C_{*}\to D_{*}
is a chain map such that

f_{n}\colon C_{n}\to D_{n}
is a cofibration in
\mathcal{A}
and the weak equivalences are the
chain homotopy equivalences. Then
\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
is homotopical thanks to the mapping
cylinder construction. Hence we can apply (
14.11
)
to the Waldhausen category
\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
and can consider its non-connective
K
-theory
spectrum
{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{A}))
.

More generally, if
\mathcal{A}
is an exact category, then the Waldhausen category
\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
can
be defined analogously and is homotopical.

Suppose that
\mathcal{W}
is a category with cofibrations
and that
\mathcal{W}
is equipped with two categories of weak equivalences, one finer than
the other,
v\mathcal{W}\subseteq w\mathcal{W}
.
Thus
\mathcal{W}
becomes a Waldhausen category in two ways.
Suppose that in both cases
\mathcal{W}
is a homotopical Walhausen category.
Let
\mathcal{W}^{w}
denote the full subcategory of
\mathcal{W}
given
by the objects
C
in
\mathcal{W}
having the property that the map
C\to\pt
belongs to
w\mathcal{W}
.
Then
\mathcal{W}^{w}
inherits two Waldhausen structures,
if we put
v\mathcal{W}^{w}=\mathcal{W}^{w}\cap v\mathcal{W}
and
w\mathcal{W}^{w}=\mathcal{W}^{w}\cap w\mathcal{W}
.
Both yield homotopical Waldhausen categories.

Theorem 14.13

(Fibration Theorem)
.

Under the assumptions above we get a weak homotopy fibration of spectra

{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\mathcal{W}^{w},v\mathcal{W}^{w})\to{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\mathcal{W},v\mathcal{W})\to{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\mathcal{W},w\mathcal{W}).

Proof.

This follows from
[
2
, Theorem 2.35]
.
∎

Theorem 14.14

(Cisinski’s Approximation Theorem)
.

Let
F\colon\mathcal{W}_{0}\to\mathcal{W}_{1}
be
an exact functor of homotopical Waldhausen categories. Assume:

(i)

An arrow in
\mathcal{W}_{0}
is a weak equivalence
in
\mathcal{W}_{0}
, if and only if its image in
\mathcal{W}_{1}
is a weak equivalence in

\mathcal{W}_{1}
;

(ii)

Given any object
C_{0}
in
\mathcal{W}_{0}
and any
map
f\colon F(C_{0})\to C_{1}
in
\mathcal{W}_{1}
, there exists a commutative diagram in
\mathcal{W}_{1}

\textstyle{F(C_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{F(u)}
\textstyle{C_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{v}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{F(D_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{w}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{D_{1}}

for a morphism
u\colon C_{0}\to D_{0}
in
\mathcal{W}_{0}
and weak equivalences
v\colon C_{1}\to D_{1}
and

w\colon F(D_{0})\to D_{1}
in
\mathcal{W}_{1}
.

Then the map of spectra
{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(F)\colon{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\mathcal{W}_{0})\xrightarrow{\simeq}{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\mathcal{W}_{1})

is a weak homotopy equivalence.

Proof.

This follows from
[
2
, Theorem 2.16]
.
∎

Theorem 14.15

(Cofinality Theorem)
.

Let
I\colon\mathcal{W}_{0}\to\mathcal{W}_{1}
be the inclusion of a full homotopical Waldhausen
subcategory
\mathcal{W}_{0}
into a homotopical Waldhausen category
\mathcal{W}_{1}
. Assume:

(i)

The functor
F

admits a mapping cylinder argument, i.e., for every morphism
f\colon C_{0}\to C_{1}

in
\mathcal{W}_{1}
such that
C_{0}
belongs to
\mathcal{W}_{0}
and
C_{1}
is the target of a weak equivalence
with some object in
\mathcal{W}_{0}
as source, there is a factorization in
\mathcal{W}_{1}

C_{0}\xrightarrow{f^{\prime}}C^{\prime}\xrightarrow{f^{\prime\prime}}C_{1}

such that
C^{\prime}
belongs to
\mathcal{W}_{0}
and
f^{\prime\prime}
is a weak equivalence;

(ii)

The category
\mathcal{W}_{1}
is dominated by
\mathcal{W}_{0}
, i.e.,
for any object
C_{1}
in
\mathcal{W}_{1}
there exists an object
C_{0}
in
\mathcal{W}_{0}
and an object
C_{1}^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{W}_{1}

and morphisms

r\colon C_{0}\to C_{1}
and
i\colon C_{1}^{\prime}\to C_{0}
such that
r\circ i
is a weak equivalence.

Then
{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(I)\colon{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\mathcal{W}_{0})\to{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
is a weak homotopy
equivalence.

Proof.

This follows from
[
2
, Theorem 2.30]
and the fact that
on the level of stable
\infty
-categories non-connective
K
-theory is inverting the
passage to the idempotent completion.
∎

For the proof of the following result we refer to
[
11
, Theorem 18.17]
.

Theorem 14.16

(The weak homotopy fibration sequence of a stable Karoubi
filtration for
K
-theory in the setting of Waldhausen categories)
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a additive category and
i\colon\mathcal{U}\to\mathcal{A}
be the inclusion
of a full additive subcategory. If the additive category
\mathcal{A}
is stably

\mathcal{U}
-filtered, then

{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{U}))\to{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{A}))\to{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{U}))

is a weak homotopy fibration of non-connective spectra,
where
{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}
has been defined in (
14.11
),

The proof of the next result can be found in
[
11
, Theorem 18.33]
.

Theorem 14.17

(Gillet-Waldhausen zigzag for non-connective
K
-theory)
.

There is a zigzag of weak homotopy equivalences,
natural in
\mathcal{A}
, from the non-connective
K
-theory spectrum
{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{A}))
in
the sense of Bunke-Kasprowski-Winges
[
2
]
to the
non-connective
K
-theory spectrum
\mathbf{K}^{\infty}(\mathcal{A})
in the sense of
Lück-Steimle
[
13
]
.

14.
j
.
The projective line

Definition 14.18

(Projective line)
.

We define
the projective line

\mathcal{X}
to be the following additive category.
Objects are
triples
(C^{+},f,C^{-})
consisting of objects
C^{+}
in
\mathcal{C}[t^{+}]
and
C^{-}
in
\mathcal{C}[t^{-}]
and an
isomorphism
f\colon j_{+}(C^{+})\to j_{-}(C^{-})
in
\mathcal{C}[t,t^{-1}]
. A morphism

(\varphi^{+},\varphi^{-})\colon(C^{+},f,C^{-})\to(D^{+},g,D^{-})
in
\mathcal{X}
consists of morphisms

\varphi^{+}\colon C^{+}\to D^{+}
in
\mathcal{C}[t]
and
\varphi^{-}\colon C^{-}\to D^{-}
in

\mathcal{C}[t^{-1}]
such that the following diagram commutes in
\mathcal{C}[t,t^{-1}]

\textstyle{j_{+}(C^{+})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{j_{+}(\varphi^{+})}
\textstyle{j_{-}(C^{-})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j_{-}(\varphi^{-})}
\textstyle{j_{+}(D^{+})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{j_{-}(D^{-}).}

Let

(14.19)

\displaystyle k^{\pm}\colon\mathcal{X}

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}[t^{\pm 1}]

be the functor sending
(C^{+},f,C^{-})
to
C^{\pm}
.

The category
\mathcal{X}
is naturally an exact category by declaring a sequence to be exact, if
and only if becomes (split) exact both after applying
k^{+}
and
k^{-}
. With this exact
structure we obtain the structure of a homotopical Waldhausen category on
\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{X})
.
Hence
{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{X}))
is defined, in contrast to
\mathbf{K}^{\infty}(\mathcal{X})

which does not make sense, since
\mathcal{X}
is neither an additive category
nor a homotopical Waldhausen category.

The next theorem will be a main ingredient in the proof of
Theorem
14.31
.

Theorem 14.20

(The algebraic
K
-theory of the projective line)
.

Consider the following (not necessarily commutative) diagram of spectra

\textstyle{{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{X}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(k^{-}))}
\scriptstyle{{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(k^{+}))}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{C}[t^{-1}]))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}\matheurm{Ch}((j_{-}))}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{C}[t]))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(j_{+}))}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{C}[t,t^{-1}])).}

There is a natural equivalence of functors

T\colon j_{+}\circ k^{+}\xrightarrow{\cong}j_{-}\circ k^{-}
, which is given on an object
(A^{+},f,A^{-})
by
f
.
It induces a preferred homotopy

{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(j_{+}))\circ{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(k^{+}))\simeq{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(j_{-}))\circ{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(k^{-}))
.

Then the diagram above is a weak homotopy pullback, i.e.,
the canonical map from
{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\mathcal{X})
to the homotopy pullback of

\textstyle{{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{C}[t^{-1}]))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}\matheurm{Ch}((j_{-}))}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{C}[t]))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(j_{+}))}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{C}[t,t^{-1}])).}

is a weak homotopy equivalence.

For the proof of Theorem
14.20
, we can use the ideas
of
[
14
, Section 5]
taking into account that meanwhile the basic
tools appearing in
[
14
, Section 4]
have been proved also for
non-connective
K
-theory.

In the first step of the proof of Theorem
14.20
we replace
the additive category
\mathcal{C}[t]
by a larger exact category
\mathcal{Y}
with equivalent

K
-theory. It is defined as follows: An object of
\mathcal{Y}
is a triple
(A^{+},f,A)

consisting of objects
A^{+}
in
\mathcal{C}[t]
and
A^{-}
of
\mathcal{C}[t,t^{-1}]
and an
isomorphism
f\colon j_{+}A^{+}\to A
in
\mathcal{C}[t,t^{-1}]
. A morphism from
(A^{+},f,A)
to

(B^{+},g,B)
is a morphism
\varphi^{+}\colon A^{+}\to B^{+}
in
\mathcal{C}[t]
and a morphism

\varphi\colon A\to B
in
\mathcal{C}[t,t^{-1}]
such that diagram in
\mathcal{C}[t,t^{-1}]

\textstyle{j_{+}(A^{+})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{j_{+}(\varphi^{+})}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi}
\textstyle{j_{+}(B^{+})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{B}

commutes. Note that
\varphi
is determined already by
\varphi^{+}
. The
category
\mathcal{Y}
is exact in the same way as
\mathcal{X}
is. Note that
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}

have the same set of objects but
\mathcal{Y}
contains more morphisms, since in contrast to

\mathcal{X}
we do not require that
\varphi
belongs to
\mathcal{C}[t^{-1}]
.

Lemma 14.21
.

The functors

a\colon\mathcal{C}[t]\to\mathcal{Y},\quad A\mapsto(A,\id,j_{-}A)

and

b\colon\mathcal{Y}\to\mathcal{C}[t],\quad(A^{+},f,A^{-})\mapsto A^{+}

are exact. The composite
b\circ a
is the identity and the composite
a\circ b
is
naturally isomorphic to the identity functor. In particular, they induce homotopy
equivalences on non-connective
K
-theory, homotopy inverse to each other,

\displaystyle{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(a))\colon{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{C}[t]))

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\simeq}

\displaystyle{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{Y}));

\displaystyle{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(b))\colon{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{Y}))

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\simeq}

\displaystyle{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{C}[t])).

Proof.

It is clear that the functors are exact. Obviously
b\circ a
is the identity. The
composite
a\circ b
is naturally isomorphic to the identity functor: the isomorphism in

\mathcal{Y}
at the object
(A^{+},f,A)
is given by

(\id,f)\colon(A^{+},\id,j_{+}A^{+})\xrightarrow{\cong}(A^{+},f,A)
. This implies

\mathbf{K}(a)\circ\mathbf{K}(b)\simeq\id
.
∎

Denote by

k^{\prime}\colon\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}

the inclusion functor, and define

j^{\prime}\colon\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{Y})\to\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{C}[t,t^{-1}]),\quad(A^{+},f,A)\mapsto A.

Then the square

\textstyle{{\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{X})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\matheurm{Ch}(k^{-})}
\scriptstyle{\matheurm{Ch}(k^{\prime})}
\textstyle{\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{C}[t^{-1}])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\matheurm{Ch}(j^{-})}
\textstyle{{\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{Y})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\matheurm{Ch}(j^{\prime})}
\textstyle{\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{C}[t,t^{-1}])}

is strictly commutative, and we are going to show that it induces a weak
homotopy pullback after applying
{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}
. To show that the square is a weak
homotopy pullback on non-connective
K
-theory, we are going to show that the
horizontal homotopy fibers of
{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(k^{-}))
and
{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(j^{\prime}))

agree up to weak homotopy equivalence.

Let
w\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{X})
be the subcategory of

\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{X})
consisting of all chain maps, which become after applying
\matheurm{Ch}(k^{-})
weak equivalences
in
\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{C}[t^{-1}])
. Let
\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{X})^{w}
be the
full subcategory of
\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{X})
of all objects, which are
w
-acyclic. In other words, an
object
(C^{+},f,C^{-})
belongs to
\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{X})^{w}
, if and only if
C^{-}
is
contractible as an
\mathcal{C}[t^{-1}]
-chain complex. Similarly, denote
by
w\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{Y})
the subcategory of all morphisms
f
such that

\matheurm{Ch}(j^{\prime})(f)
is a chain homotopy equivalence in
\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{C}[t,t^{-1}])
, and adopt the
notation
\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{Y})^{w}
for the
w
-acyclic objects.

Lemma 14.22
.

The maps

\displaystyle{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(k^{-}))\colon

\displaystyle{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{X}),w)\to{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{C}[t^{-1}]));

\displaystyle{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(j^{\prime}))\colon

\displaystyle{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{Y}),w)\to{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{C}[t,t^{-1}])),

are homotopy equivalences.

Proof.

We want to apply the Cisinki’s Approximation Theorem
14.14
.
We give the details only for
{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(k^{-}))
, the analogous proof for
{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(j^{\prime}))
is
left to the reader. We have to verify the
conditions
(i)
and
(ii)

appearing in Cisinki’s Approximation
Theorem
14.14
.

A morphism
f
in
\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{X})
is by definition in
w\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{X})
, if and only if

\matheurm{Ch}(k^{-})(f)
is a chain homotopy equivalence in
\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{C}[t^{-1}])
. This takes care of
condition
(i)
for
{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(k^{-}))
.

Next we deal with condition
(ii)
. Consider an object

(C^{+},f,C^{-})
in
\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{X})
and a morphism
\varphi^{-}\colon C^{-}\to D^{-}
in

\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{C}[t^{-1}])
. We will extend
\varphi^{-}
to a morphism

\varphi=(\varphi^{+},\varphi^{-})\colon(C^{+},f,C^{-})\to(D^{+},g,D^{-})

in
\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{X})
. If we have achieved this, we are done by the following argument.
Note that
\varphi=(\varphi^{+},\varphi^{-})
is a morphism in
\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{X})
projecting
to
\varphi^{-}
under
\matheurm{Ch}(k^{-})
. Then, factorizing
\varphi=\mu\circ\psi
into a
cofibration
\psi
followed by a weak equivalence
\mu
(using the mapping cylinder), we
can write
\varphi^{-}=\mu^{-}\circ\matheurm{Ch}(k^{-})(\psi)
, where
\psi
is a cofibration and

\mu^{-}
is a weak equivalence, as required in
condition
(ii)
.

The construction of
D^{+}
and
\varphi^{+}
require the following preparation.

Consider an object
\underline{A}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*})
and an element

\underline{k}=\{k_{m}\mid m\in\mathbb{N}\}\in\prod_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbb{Z}
. Define a morphism

t^{\underline{k}}\colon\underline{A}\to\underline{A}
in

\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*}[t,t^{-1}])
by
(t^{k_{m}})_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
, where

t^{k_{m}}\colon A_{m}\to A_{m}
is the obvious morphism in
\mathcal{A}_{m}[t,t^{-1}]
determined
by
t^{k_{m}}
. We can and will regard

t^{\underline{k}}\colon\underline{A}\to\underline{A}
also as a morphism in

\mathcal{C}[t,t^{-1}]
. One easily checks for any morphism
f\colon A\to B
in

\mathcal{C}[t,t^{-1}]
that
t^{\underline{k}}\circ f=f\circ t^{\underline{k}}
holds.
Moreover we have

t^{\underline{k}+\underline{k^{\prime}}}=t^{\underline{k}}\circ t^{\underline{k^{\prime}}}
for

\underline{k}+\underline{k^{\prime}}
defined by the componentwise addition in

\prod_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbb{Z}
and
t^{\underline{0}}=\id
for

\underline{0}\in\prod_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbb{Z}
given by the element which has zero as entry for
the component for
m\in\mathbb{N}
. The following property is important for us. Given a
morphism
f\colon A\to B
in
\mathcal{C}[t,t^{-1}]
, there exists

\underline{k}_{f}\in\prod_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbb{Z}
such that for every

\underline{k}\in\prod_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbb{Z}
with
\underline{k}\geq\underline{k}_{f}
we have

t^{\underline{k}}\circ f
in
\mathcal{C}[t]
, where
\underline{k}\geq\underline{k}_{f}
is
to be understood componentwise.

Choose a natural number
N
such that

C^{+}_{m}=D^{-}_{m}=0
for
|m|>N
. Consider
\underline{k}\in\prod_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbb{Z}
.
For any integer
n
define
n\cdot\underline{k}
to be the element, whose
i
-th entry is
n\cdot k_{m}
.
Then we obtain the following commutative diagram in
\mathcal{C}[t,t^{-1}]

\textstyle{\vdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\vdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\vdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\id}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\id}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{D^{-}_{N}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\scriptstyle{t^{-\underline{k}}}

\scriptstyle{\id}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{0}
\scriptstyle{\id}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{0}
\textstyle{\vdots}
\textstyle{\vdots}
\textstyle{\vdots}

where the left column is the chain complex
C^{+}
, the right column is the chain
complex
D^{-}
and the middle column is obtained from
D^{-}
by composing each
differential with
t^{\underline{k}}
. Since there are only finitely many objects
and morphisms in the diagram above, which are different from zero, we can choose

\underline{k}
such that each horizontal arrow from the first column to the
second column and each vertical arrow in the middle column belong to
\mathcal{C}[t]
.
Hence the middle column defines a chain complex
D^{+}
over
\mathcal{C}[t]
, the
horizontal arrows from the left column to the middle column define a chain map

\phi^{+}\colon C^{+}\to D^{+}
over
\mathcal{C}[t]
and the collection of the morphisms from
the middle column to the right column define a chain homotopy equivalence

g\colon D^{+}\to D^{-}
over
\mathcal{C}[t,t^{-1}]
. By construction we have

j_{-}(\varphi^{-})\circ f=g\circ j_{+}(\varphi^{+})
.
This finishes the construction of
the desired morphism
\phi
and hence the proof of
Lemma
14.22
.
∎

Theorem 14.23
.

There are weak homotopy fibration sequences

\displaystyle{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{X})^{w})

\displaystyle\to{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{X}))\to{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\mathcal{C}[t^{-1}]);

\displaystyle{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{Y})^{w})

\displaystyle\to{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{Y}))\to{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\mathcal{C}[t,t^{-1}]).

Proof.

We give the
details only for the first sequence, the analogous proof for the second one is
left to the reader.

We apply the Fibration Theorem
14.13
in the case

\mathcal{W}=\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{X})
,
w
as described above and
v
the structure of weak equivalences
coming from chain homotopy equivalences. The necessary conditions appearing in the
Fibration Theorem
14.13
are satisfied by
[
14
, Lemma 3.1 and
Lemma 4.12]
. Thus we obtain a weak homotopy
fibration of spectra

{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{X})^{w})\to{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{X}))\to{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{X}),w).

Because of Lemma
14.22

we obtain a weak homotopy fibration

{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{X})^{w})\to{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{X}))\to{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{C}[t^{-1}])).

∎

Lemma 14.24
.

The functor
k^{\prime}
induces a weak homotopy equivalence

{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{X})^{w})\xrightarrow{\simeq}{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{Y})^{w}).

Proof.

Again we will use the Cisinki’s Approximation Theorem
14.14
.
We have to verify the
conditions
(i)
and
(ii)

appearing in Cisinki’s Approximation
Theorem
14.14
.

Let

(14.25)

\textstyle{j_{+}C^{+}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{j_{+}\varphi^{+}}
\textstyle{j_{-}C^{-}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j_{-}\varphi^{-}}
\textstyle{j_{+}D^{+}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{j_{-}D^{-}}

represent a morphism in
\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{X})^{w}
, which maps to a weak equivalence in

\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{Y})^{w}
. Then
\varphi^{+}
is a chain homotopy equivalence in

\mathcal{C}[t]
and
\varphi^{-}
is a chain homotopy equivalence in

\mathcal{C}[t,t^{-1}]
. By assumption,
C^{-}
and
D^{-}
are contractible in
\mathcal{C}[t^{-1}]
, so

\varphi^{-}
has to be an equivalence in
\mathcal{C}[t^{-1}]
. It follows that the
morphism given by (
14.25
) is a weak equivalence
in
\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{X})^{w}
already. This takes care of
condition
(i)
.

It remains to check condition
(ii)
.
Suppose now that

(14.26)

\textstyle{j_{+}C^{+}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{j_{+}\varphi^{+}}
\textstyle{C^{-}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi^{-}}
\textstyle{j_{+}D^{+}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{D^{-}}

represents a morphism in

\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{Y})^{w}
satisfying
(C^{+},f,C^{-})
in
\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{X})^{w}
. We have to
factor this morphism through a map in
\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{X})^{w}
(which we may then
replace by a cofibration using the mapping cylinder) and a weak
equivalence in
\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{Y})^{w}
.

Note that the morphism
\varphi^{-}
is a chain homotopy equivalence in

\mathcal{C}[t,t^{-1}]
, as both
C^{-}
and
D^{-}
are contractible in that
category by assumption. We conclude from
[
14
, Lemma 3.1 (ix)]

that there is a chain isomorphism of the shape

\begin{pmatrix}\varphi^{-}&y\\
x&z\end{pmatrix}\colon C^{-}\oplus E\xrightarrow{\cong}D^{-}\oplus E^{\prime},

where
E
and
E^{\prime}
are elementary chain complexes in

\mathcal{C}[t,t^{-1}]
, or even in
\mathcal{C}
, since both categories have the
same objects.

For appropriate
\underline{k}\in\prod_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbb{Z}
, the commutative diagram

\textstyle{j_{+}C^{+}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{\footnotesize\begin{pmatrix}j_{+}\varphi^{+}\\
t^{\underline{k}}\circ x\circ f\end{pmatrix}}
\textstyle{C^{-}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\footnotesize\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\end{pmatrix}}
\textstyle{j_{+}D^{+}\oplus i_{0}E^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\footnotesize\begin{pmatrix}g^{-1}\circ\varphi^{-}&g^{-1}\circ y\\
t^{\underline{k}}\circ x&t^{\underline{k}}\circ z\end{pmatrix}}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\footnotesize\begin{pmatrix}1&0\end{pmatrix}}
\textstyle{C^{-}\oplus i_{0}E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\footnotesize\begin{pmatrix}\varphi^{-}&y\end{pmatrix}}
\textstyle{j_{+}D^{+}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{D^{-}}

provides the desired factorization of (
14.26
).
∎

Proof of Theorem
14.20
.

Theorem
14.20
follows from
Lemma
14.21
, Lemma
14.23
, and Lemma
14.24
.
∎

14.
k
.
The global section functor

Recall that we have defined truncation functors in Subsection
14.h
.

Definition 14.27

(Global section functor)
.

The
global section functor

\Gamma\colon\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{X})\to\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{C}^{\kappa})

sends an object
(C^{+},f,C^{-})
to the
\mathcal{C}^{\kappa}
-chain complex

\Sigma^{-1}\cone\bigl(f[\,]\colon C^{+}[0,\infty]\to C^{-}[1,\infty]\bigr).

A morphism

(\varphi^{+},\varphi^{-})\colon(C^{+},f,C^{-})\to(D^{+},g,D^{-})
of
\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{X})

is sent to the morphism in
\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{A}^{\kappa})
obtained from
the commutative diagram (using the trivial homotopy)

\textstyle{C^{+}[0,\infty]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f[\,]}
\scriptstyle{\varphi^{+}[\,]}
\textstyle{C^{-}[-1,\infty]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi^{-}[\,]}
\textstyle{D^{+}[0,\infty]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g[\,]}
\textstyle{D^{-}[-1,\infty].}

Note that
f[\,]
is indeed a chain map, as it is the composite of the three chain maps

C^{+}[0,\infty]\xrightarrow{\id[\,]}C^{+}[-\infty,\infty]\xrightarrow{i^{0}f=f[\,]}C^{-}[-\infty,\infty]\xrightarrow{\id[\,]}C^{-}[-1,\infty]

Let
\matheurm{Ch}^{\operatorname{hf}}(\mathcal{C})\subset\matheurm{Ch}(\Idem(\mathcal{C}^{\kappa}))
be the full
subcategory of homotopy finite chain complexes over
\Idem(\mathcal{C}^{\kappa})
, i.e.,
chain complexes over
\Idem(\mathcal{C}^{\kappa})
,
which are homotopy equivalent to a bounded chain complex over
\Idem(\mathcal{C})
.

It follows
from
[
14
, Lemma 3.5]
that this category is closed under pushouts
along a cofibration, so it is a Waldhausen subcategory of
\matheurm{Ch}(\Idem(\mathcal{C}^{\kappa}))
. The
Approximation Theorem
14.14
of Cisinski shows that the inclusion

I(\mathcal{C})\colon\matheurm{Ch}(\Idem(\mathcal{C}))\to\matheurm{Ch}^{\operatorname{hf}}(\mathcal{C})

induces an equivalence

(14.28)

{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(I(\mathcal{C}))\colon{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(\Idem(\mathcal{C})))\xrightarrow{\sim}{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}^{\operatorname{hf}}(\mathcal{C}))

on non-connective
K
-theory.

Lemma 14.29
.

(i)

The functor
\Gamma
is Waldhausen exact (for the canonical Waldhausen structures);

(ii)

For any object
(C^{+},f,C^{-})
of

\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{X})
, the chain complex given by the projective line

\Gamma(C^{+},f,C^{-})\in\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{C}^{\kappa})\subseteq\matheurm{Ch}(\Idem(\mathcal{C}^{\kappa}))
is
chain homotopy equivalent to an object in
\matheurm{Ch}(\Idem(\mathcal{C}))
.

Thus,
\Gamma
defines a Waldhausen
exact functor

\Gamma(\mathcal{C})\colon\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{X})\to\matheurm{Ch}^{\operatorname{hf}}(\mathcal{C}).

Proof.

(i)

It is not hard to check using
[
14
, Section 4.1]
that the two functors

k^{\pm}\colon\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{X})\to\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{C}[t^{\pm 1}])
are Waldhausen exact. The restriction functors
from
\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{C}[t])
,
\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{C}[t^{-1}])
and

\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{C}[t,t^{-1}])
to
\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{C}^{\kappa})
are defined on the level of
additive categories and hence are Waldhausen exact. Taking cones and suspensions is also
Waldhausen exact.

(ii)

Write the morphism
f_{n}^{-1}\colon C^{+}_{n}\to C^{-}_{n}
in
\mathcal{C}[t,t^{-1}]
as a sum

\sum_{l_{n}=a_{n}}^{b_{n}}f_{n}[l_{n}]\cdot s^{l_{n}}
for appropriate morphisms

f_{n}[l_{n}]\colon\mathcal{S}(\Phi[t,t^{-1}])^{l_{n}}(C^{-}_{n})\to C^{+}_{n}
in
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*}[t,t^{-1}])
. Note
that
f_{n}[l_{n}]=(f_{n}[l_{n}]_{m})_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
for appropriate morphisms

f_{n}[l_{n}]_{m}\colon\Phi^{l_{n}}((C^{-}_{n})_{m})\to(C^{+}_{n})_{m}
in
\mathcal{A}_{0}[t,t^{-1}]
. Now choose for

n\in\mathbb{Z}
,
l_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}
, and
m\in\mathbb{N}
a natural number
k_{n}[l]_{m}
such that we can
write

f_{n}[l_{n}]_{m}=\sum_{j_{n,l_{n},m}=-k_{n}[l_{n}]_{m}}^{k_{n}[l_{n}]_{m}}f_{n}[l_{n}]_{m}[j_{n,l_{n},m}]\cdot t^{j_{n,l_{n},m}}

for appropriate morphisms

f_{n}[l_{n}]_{m}[j_{n,l_{n},m}]\colon\Phi^{l_{n}}((C^{-}_{n})_{m})\to(C^{+}_{n})_{m}
in
\mathcal{A}_{0}
. Since

C^{+}
and
C^{-}
are bounded, there exists a natural number
N
such that
C^{-}_{n}=0

and
C^{+}_{n}=0
holds for
|n|>N
. Hence we get for every
n\in\mathbb{Z}
with
|n|>N
and
every
m\in\mathbb{N}
that
(C^{-}_{n})_{m}=0
and
(C^{+}_{n})_{m}=0
hold. Now define for
m\in\mathbb{N}

a natural number
k_{i}
by

k_{m}=1+\max\{k_{n}[l_{n}]_{m}\mid-N\leq n\leq N,a_{n}\leq l_{n}\leq b_{n}\}.

This definition makes sense since the set
\bigcup_{n=-N}^{N}\{l_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}\mid a_{n}\leq l_{n}\leq b_{n}\}
is finite. Note that

k_{m}
depends only on
m
, in contrast to
k_{n}[l_{n}]_{m}
. We conclude that

f_{n}[l_{n}]_{m}=\sum_{j_{n,l_{n},m}=-k_{m}+1}^{k_{m}-1}f_{n}[l_{n}]_{m}[j_{n,l_{n},m}]\cdot t^{j_{n,l_{n},m}}
holds for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
.

Define
\underline{k}\in\prod_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbb{Z}
by the collection
\{k_{m}\mid m\in\mathbb{N}\}
.
Then
{f^{-1}}[\,]\colon C^{-}[\underline{-\infty},\underline{\infty}]\to C^{+}[\underline{k},\underline{\infty}]

factorizes as

\textstyle{C^{-}[\underline{-\infty},\underline{\infty}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{-1}[\,]}
\scriptstyle{\id[\,]}
\textstyle{C^{+}[\underline{k},\underline{\infty}]}
\textstyle{C^{-}[\underline{1},\underline{\infty}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{f^{-1}}}

and the composite

C^{+}[\underline{k},\underline{\infty}]\xrightarrow{f[\,]}C^{-}[\underline{1},\underline{\infty}]\xrightarrow{\overline{f^{-1}}}C^{+}[\underline{k},\infty]

is the identity map.

Hence over
\Idem(\mathcal{C}^{\kappa})
, the chain complex
C^{-}[1,\infty]
splits as

C^{-}[1,\infty]\cong C^{+}[\underline{k},\underline{\infty}]\oplus R,

where
R_{n}
is given by
(C^{-}_{n}[\underline{1},\underline{\infty}],\id-p_{n})
for the projection

p_{n}\colon C^{-}[\underline{1},\underline{\infty}]\xrightarrow{\overline{f^{-1}}}C^{+}[\underline{k},\infty]\xrightarrow{f[\,]}C^{-}[\underline{1},\underline{\infty}].

Since the composite

C^{-}[2\cdot\underline{k},\underline{\infty}]\xrightarrow{f^{-1}[\,]}C^{+}[\underline{k},\underline{\infty}]\xrightarrow{f[\,]}C^{-}[2\cdot\underline{k},\underline{\infty}]

is the identity,
\id-p_{n}
restricted to
C^{-}[2\cdot\underline{k},\underline{\infty}]

is trivial. Hence we can find a projection

q_{n}\colon C^{-}_{n}[\underline{1},2\cdot\underline{k}-\underline{1}]\to C^{-}_{n}[\underline{1},2\cdot\underline{k}-\underline{1}]
such that
R_{n}
and

(C^{-}_{n}[\underline{1},2\cdot\underline{k}-\underline{1}],q_{n})
are isomorphic. Since

C^{-}_{n}[\underline{1},2\cdot\underline{k}-\underline{1}]
belongs to
\mathcal{C}
,

R_{n}
is isomorphic to an object in
\Idem(\mathcal{C})

for every
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. This implies that the bounded
\Idem(\mathcal{C}^{\kappa})
-chain complex
R
is
isomorphic to a bounded
\Idem(\mathcal{C})
-chain complex.
We obtain an exact sequence of
\Idem(\mathcal{C}^{\kappa})
-chain complexes

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C^{+}[\underline{k},\infty]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\id}
\textstyle{C^{+}[0,\infty]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{-f[\,]}
\textstyle{C^{+}[0,\underline{k}-\underline{1}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C^{+}[\underline{k},\infty]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{-f[\,]}
\textstyle{C^{-}[1,\infty]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{r}
\textstyle{R\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

where
r_{n}\colon C^{-}_{n}[1,\infty]\to R_{n}
is the canonical projection given by
\id-p_{n}

and
g
is the induced map on the quotients. We conclude that
\Sigma^{-1}\cone(-f[\,])\simeq\Sigma^{-1}\cone(g)
, which is isomorphic to a chain complex in
\Idem(\mathcal{C})
. Hence
\Gamma(C^{+},f,C^{-})
belongs to

\matheurm{Ch}^{\operatorname{hf}}(\mathcal{C})
.
∎

14.
l
.
Embedding lower-K-theory in higher
K
-theory

For
k\in\mathbb{Z}
and a spectrum
\mathbf{E}
, let

\Sigma^{k}\mathbf{E}
be the spectrum obtained by shifting, i.e.,

(\Sigma^{k}\mathbf{E})_{n}=\mathbf{E}_{n-k}
. Let

(14.30)

\mathbf{d}(\mathbf{E})\colon\Sigma^{-1}S^{1}_{+}\wedge\mathbf{E}\to\mathbf{E}

be the weak homotopy equivalence, natural in
\mathbf{E}
, which is given in dimension
n
by the
n
-th
structure map of
\mathbf{E}
. Let
i\colon S^{1}\to S^{1}\vee S^{1}
be the pinching map. For
a spectrum
\mathbf{E}
, define a map of spectra

\nabla\colon\Sigma^{–1}S^{1}_{+}\wedge\mathbf{E}\to(\Sigma^{–1}S^{1}_{+}\wedge\mathbf{E})\vee(\Sigma^{–1}S^{1}_{+}\wedge\mathbf{E}),

natural in
\mathbf{E}
, by

(\Sigma^{–1}S^{1}_{+}\wedge\mathbf{E})_{n}=S^{1}_{+}\wedge E_{n+1}\xrightarrow{i_{+}\wedge E_{n+1}}(S^{1}\vee S^{1})_{+}\wedge E_{n+1}\\
\xrightarrow{\cong}(S^{1}_{+}\wedge E_{n+1})\vee(S^{1}_{+}\wedge E_{n+1})=(\Sigma^{-1}S^{1}_{+}\wedge\mathbf{E})_{n}\vee(\Sigma^{-1}S^{1}_{+}\wedge\mathbf{E})_{n}.

The next theorem essentially says that

S^{1}_{+}\wedge{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{C}))
is up to weak homotopy equivalence
a retract of
{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{C}[t,t^{-1}]))
.

Theorem 14.31
.

There is a diagram of spectra

\textstyle{S^{1}_{+}\wedge{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{C}))}
\textstyle{S^{1}_{+}\wedge(\Sigma^{-1}S^{1}_{+}\wedge{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{C})))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\id_{S^{1}_{+}}\wedge\mathbf{d}({\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{C})))}
\scriptstyle{\mathbf{s}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\mathbf{i}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{H}(\mathcal{C})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\mathbf{f}}
\scriptstyle{\mathbf{r}}
\textstyle{S^{1}_{+}\wedge\bigl(\Sigma^{-1}S^{1}_{+}\wedge{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}^{\operatorname{hf}}(\mathcal{C}))\bigr),}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{C}[t,t^{-1}]))}

such that the triangle commutes up to a preferred homotopy
\mathbf{h}
and the maps marked
with
\simeq
are weak homotopy equivalences. Moreover, everything including the
preferred homotopy
\mathbf{h}
is natural in
\Phi
.

For its proof we need the next Lemma
14.33
,

We define for
j=0,1
a functor of additive categories

(14.32)

L_{j}\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{X}

as follows. The functor
L_{j}(\mathcal{C})
sends an object
C
in
\mathcal{C}
to the object

(C,t^{\underline{j}},C)
in
\mathcal{X}
, where
\underline{j}\in\prod_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbb{Z}
is
given by the constant function
I\to\mathbb{Z}
with value
j
. A morphism
u\colon C\to C^{\prime}

in
\mathcal{C}
is sent to the morphism from
(C,t^{\underline{j}},C)
to

(C^{\prime},t^{\underline{j}},C^{\prime})
in
\mathcal{X}
given by the morphism
j_{+}(u)\colon C\to C
in

\mathcal{C}[t]
and the morphism
j_{-}(u)\colon C\to C
in
\mathcal{C}[t^{-1}]
. Let
J(C)\colon\mathcal{C}\to\Idem(\mathcal{C})

and
I(\mathcal{C})\colon\matheurm{Ch}(\Idem(\mathcal{C}))\to\matheurm{Ch}^{\operatorname{hf}}(\mathcal{C})
be the obvious inclusions and

0\colon\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{C})\to\matheurm{Ch}^{\operatorname{hf}}(\mathcal{C})
be the constant functor with value the chain
complex, all of whose chain objects are
0
.

Lemma 14.33
.

(i)

There is a natural equivalence
of functors
\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{C})\to\matheurm{Ch}^{\operatorname{hf}}(\mathcal{C})

T_{0}\colon I(\mathcal{C})\circ\matheurm{Ch}(J(C))\xrightarrow{\simeq}\Gamma\circ\matheurm{Ch}(L_{0}),

which is natural in
\Phi
;

(ii)

There is a natural equivalence of functors
\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{C})\to\matheurm{Ch}^{\operatorname{hf}}(\mathcal{C})

T_{1}\colon 0\xrightarrow{\simeq}\Gamma\circ\matheurm{Ch}(L_{1}),

which is natural in
\Phi
;

Proof.

(i)
Fix an object
C
in

\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{C})
. Then
\Gamma(L_{0}(C))
is by definition

\Sigma^{-1}\cone\bigl(\id[\,]\colon C[0,\infty]\to C[1,\infty]\bigr).

We have the obvious split exact sequence in

\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{C}^{\kappa})

0\to C[\underline{0},\underline{0}]\xrightarrow{\id[\,]}C[\underline{0},\underline{\infty}]\xrightarrow{\id[\,]}C[\underline{1},\underline{\infty}]\to 0.

It induces a chain homotopy equivalence in
\matheurm{Ch}^{\operatorname{hf}}(\mathcal{C})

T_{0}(C)\colon C\xrightarrow{\simeq}\Gamma(L_{0}(C)).

(ii)

Fix an object
C
in
\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{C})
. Then
\Gamma(L_{1}(C))
is by definition

\Sigma^{-1}\cone\bigl(t^{\underline{1}}[\,]\colon C[0,\infty]\to C[1,\infty]\bigr).

The chain map
t^{\underline{1}}[\,]\colon C[0,\infty]\to C[1,\infty]

is a chain isomorphism, its inverse is

t^{\underline{-1}}[\,]\colon C[\underline{1},\underline{\infty}]\to C[\underline{0},\underline{\infty}]
.
Hence
\Gamma(L_{1}(C))
is contractible and we obtain a chain homotopy equivalence in
\matheurm{Ch}^{\operatorname{hf}}(\mathcal{C})

T_{1}(C)\colon 0\xrightarrow{\simeq}\Gamma(L_{0}(C)).

∎

Proof of Theorem
14.31
.

Denote by
\ast
the trivial spectrum.
Consider the following diagram of non-connective
spectra

\textstyle{\ast\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S\wedge{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{C}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\nabla}
\textstyle{\ast\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(S\wedge{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{C})))\vee(S\wedge{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{C})))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbf{a}}
\textstyle{S\wedge{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{C}[t]))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S\wedge{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{X}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S\wedge{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\Gamma)}
\scriptstyle{\mathbf{b}^{+}}
\scriptstyle{\mathbf{b}^{-}}
\textstyle{S\wedge{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{C}[t^{-1}]))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ast}
\textstyle{S\wedge{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}^{\operatorname{hf}}(\mathcal{C}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ast}

where we abbreviate
S=\Sigma^{-1}S^{1}_{+}
,
u\colon S^{1}\to S^{1}
sends
z
to

z^{-1}
, and the maps
\mathbf{a}
and
\mathbf{b}^{\pm}
are given by

\displaystyle\mathbf{a}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle(\Sigma^{-1}\id_{S^{1}_{+}}\wedge\mathbf{K}(\matheurm{Ch}(L_{0})))\vee(\Sigma^{-1}u\wedge{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(L_{1})));

\displaystyle\mathbf{b}^{\pm}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\Sigma^{-1}\id_{S^{1}_{+}}\wedge{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(k^{\pm})).

Note that
u
is a base point preserving selfmap of
S^{1}
of degree
-1
. Fix a
pointed nullhomotopy for the composite

S^{1}\xrightarrow{i}S^{1}\vee S^{1}\xrightarrow{\id_{S^{1}}\vee u}S^{1}
. Since

k^{\pm}\circ L_{0}
and
k^{\pm}\circ L_{1}
agree, both are equal to with
i^{\pm}
, the
upper left square and the upper right square commute up to a preferred homotopy,
natural in
\Phi
. The lower left and the lower right square commute. The homotopy
pushout of the upper row is

S^{1}_{+}\wedge(\Sigma^{-1}S^{1}_{+}\wedge{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{C})))

and of the lower row is
S^{1}_{+}\wedge(\Sigma^{-1}S^{1}_{+}\wedge{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}^{\operatorname{hf}}\mathcal{C}))
. Let

\mathbf{HP}(\mathcal{C})
be the homotopy pushout of the middle row. Then we get from the data above maps of
spectra

\displaystyle\mathbf{s}\colon S^{1}_{+}\wedge(\Sigma^{-1}S^{1}_{+}\wedge{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{C})))

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\mathbf{HP}(\mathcal{C});

\displaystyle\mathbf{r}\colon\mathbf{HP}(\mathcal{C})

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle S^{1}_{+}\wedge\bigl(\Sigma^{-1}S^{1}_{+}\wedge{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}^{\operatorname{hf}}(\mathcal{C}))\bigr).

The canonical inclusion
J(C)\colon\mathcal{C}\to\Idem(\mathcal{C})
induces a weak homotopy
equivalence

{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(J(\mathcal{C})))\colon{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{C}))\xrightarrow{\simeq}{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(\Idem(\mathcal{C})))
. This follows from Theorem
14.15
,
where the elementary proof that the conditions appearing in
Theorem
14.15
are satisfied is left to the reader. Define the weak
homotopy equivalence

\mathbf{i}=\colon S^{1}_{+}\wedge\bigl(\Sigma^{-1}S^{1}_{+}\wedge{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{C}))\bigr)\xrightarrow{S^{1}_{+}\wedge(\Sigma^{-1}S^{1}_{+}\wedge{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(J(C))))}\\
S^{1}_{+}\wedge\bigl(\Sigma^{-1}S^{1}_{+}\wedge{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(\Idem(\mathcal{C})))\bigr)\\
\xrightarrow{\id_{S^{1}_{+}}\wedge\Sigma^{-1}\id_{S^{1}_{+}}\wedge{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(I(\mathcal{C}))}S^{1}_{+}\wedge\bigl(\Sigma^{-1}S^{1}_{+}\wedge{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}^{\operatorname{hf}}(\mathcal{C}))\bigr),

where
{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(I(\mathcal{C}))~
is the weak homotopy equivalence
of (
14.28
).

The composite

\mathbf{r}\circ\mathbf{s}\colon S^{1}_{+}\wedge(\Sigma^{-1}S^{1}_{+}\wedge{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{C})))\to S^{1}_{+}\wedge\bigl(\Sigma^{-1}S^{1}_{+}\wedge{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}^{\operatorname{hf}}(\mathcal{C}))\bigr)

is homotopic to
\mathbf{i}
by Lemma
14.33
.
From the commutative diagram

\textstyle{\Sigma^{-1}S^{1}_{+}\wedge{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{C}[t]))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbf{d}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{C}[t]))}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{-1}S^{1}_{+}\wedge{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{X}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma^{-1}\id_{S^{1}_{+}}\wedge{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(k^{+}))}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma^{-1}\id_{S^{1}_{+}}\wedge{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(k^{-}))}
\scriptstyle{\mathbf{d}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{X}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(k^{+}))}
\scriptstyle{{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(k^{-}))}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{-1}S^{1}_{+}\wedge{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{C}[t^{-1}]))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbf{d}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{C}[t^{-1}]))}

and Theorem
14.20
, we obtain a weak homotopy equivalence of
spectra, natural in
\Phi
,

\mathbf{f}\colon\mathbf{HP}(\mathcal{C})\xrightarrow{\simeq}{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{C}[t,t^{-1}])).

We get from the weak homotopy equivalence
\mathbf{d}({\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{C})))
of (
14.30
) a weak
homotopy equivalence

\id_{S^{1}_{+}}\wedge\mathbf{d}({\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{C})))\colon S^{1}_{+}\wedge\bigl(\Sigma^{-1}S^{1}_{+}\wedge{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{C}))\bigr)\xrightarrow{\simeq}S^{1}_{+}\wedge{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{C})).

This finishes the proof of Theorem
14.31
.
∎

.

It is not true that

These homotopies are all induced by obvious natural

\widehat{\Phi}=\mathcal{S}(\Phi)\colon\widehat{\mathcal{C}}=\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*})\xrightarrow{\cong}\widehat{\mathcal{C}}=\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{*})

.

Next we explain, how we get the corresponding diagram for

such that the composite of the left two vertical arrows and the composite of the right two

.

The point is that the construction of map from the diagram (

) to

.
Now diagram (

).

Recall that
{\mathbf{K}}^{\infty,\operatorname{W}}(\matheurm{Ch}(\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{*})))

It determines a
